ARE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case Study PCM_BO_C1

Meridian Design Group is a 58-person architecture firm organized as a professional corporation (PC) in a state that requires a majority of shareholders to hold architecture licenses. The firm's five principals are evaluating a Statement of Qualifications submission for a federally funded transit hub project procured under Qualifications-Based Selection. At the same time, the firm's HR director is reviewing whether newly promoted job captains, who do not hold an architecture license and earn a salary below the firm's usual exempt threshold, should be reclassified as non-exempt for overtime purposes. Two of the five principals, both in their early sixties, have also asked the remaining partners to begin discussing an ownership transition timeline. The managing principal must balance the pursuit's staffing demands, the compliance question, and the succession conversation within the same fiscal quarter.

  1. The correct answer is C (State boards restrict ownership of licensed-practice corporations to ensure professional judgment remains controlled by licensed practitioners): Many state licensing boards require that a controlling share, often a majority, of a professional corporation's ownership be held by individuals licensed in the regulated profession so that licensed judgment, not outside investors, governs firm decisions. This protects the public interest embedded in licensure laws. Option A is wrong because incorporation never removes a firm from board oversight, B is wrong because ownership rules apply to corporate and LLC-type entities specifically and not only sole proprietorships, and D is wrong because a PC structure limits liability for business debts but never for an individual's own professional negligence.
  2. The correct answer is A (The agency must rank firms by qualifications and select the most qualified firm first): Under the Brooks Act, federal agencies must evaluate architecture and engineering firms solely on qualifications, rank them, and only then negotiate a fair and reasonable fee with the top-ranked firm before any price is discussed. If negotiations fail, the agency moves to the next-ranked firm rather than reopening price competition. Option B describes prohibited low-bid procurement, C confuses fee negotiation timing with project phasing, and D describes an averaging method that has no basis in QBS procedure.
  3. The correct answer is B (The job captains must be classified as non-exempt and paid overtime for hours worked beyond 40 per week): The FLSA professional exemption generally requires both a duties test and a minimum salary threshold, and an employee paid below that threshold cannot be treated as exempt no matter how professional the work appears. Employers must therefore track hours and pay overtime for any hours worked beyond 40 in a week for staff who fail the salary test. Option A wrongly assumes duties alone control the outcome, C is wrong because licensure is not the sole exemption test, and D incorrectly dismisses the salary threshold entirely.
  4. The correct answer is D (Standard Form 330, Architect-Engineer Qualifications): SF330 is the federal government's standard qualifications submission, with Part I presenting contract-specific team qualifications and Part II presenting the firm's general qualifications, and it is the customary format for responding to a QBS request for qualifications. Option A and B name contract and general-conditions documents unrelated to the qualifications submission stage, and C names a timekeeping form with no marketing function.
  5. The correct answer is C (Available internal capital, remaining principals' readiness to lead, and desired continuity of firm culture): Succession planning decisions weigh whether remaining or rising leaders can finance and manage a buyout, whether staff ownership can be structured and funded, and whether the firm's culture and client relationships would best survive an internal transition versus a merger into another practice. Option A lists irrelevant factors, B oversimplifies a multi-factor decision to firm age alone, and D gives undue weight to a party with no ownership stake in the decision.
  6. The correct answer is B (Correct the wage and hour compliance issue promptly, since it carries legal exposure independent of other priorities): A known FLSA misclassification creates ongoing legal and financial exposure for unpaid overtime that compounds the longer it continues, so it should be addressed on its own timeline rather than deferred behind unrelated business decisions. The QBS pursuit and succession planning are important but do not carry the same regulatory liability clock. Option A risks accumulating back-pay liability, C is premature and could destabilize partner relationships before terms are settled, and D unnecessarily sacrifices a legitimate business opportunity with no real conflict against fixing the compliance issue.
  7. The correct answer is A (A sole proprietorship, which exposes the owner's personal assets to every firm obligation): A sole proprietorship requires no separate state formation filing and creates no legal separation between the owner and the business, so all business debts and professional liabilities fall directly on the individual owner. This makes it the fastest structure to begin but the riskiest for personal asset protection. Option B is incorrect because even a single-member LLC generally requires a state filing and provides liability protection, C requires formal incorporation paperwork, and D cannot exist with only one owner since a partnership requires at least two people.
  8. The correct answer is D (Each partner is jointly and severally liable, so any partner's personal assets are at risk): In a general partnership with no liability-limiting election, partners share joint and several liability for the firm's obligations, meaning any partner's personal assets can be pursued for a claim regardless of which partner directly caused it. This is the central liability risk that leads many design practices to choose an LLP, LLC, or PC instead. Option A wrongly limits liability to the signing partner alone, B invents a liability cap that does not exist in a general partnership, and C incorrectly assumes insurance eliminates personal exposure entirely.
  9. The correct answers are A, B, and C: An LLC or PLLC generally shields members from personal liability for the firm's business debts, passes income through to members' individual tax returns to avoid corporate double taxation, and in many states must take the specific PLLC form when the members are licensed design professionals. Option D is wrong because forming an LLC does not confer a license on anyone, E is wrong because liability protection for ordinary business debts is the structure's defining feature, and F is wrong because no LLC or PLLC variant shields a member from liability for that member's own professional negligence.
  10. The correct answer is B (A professional limited liability company, formed specifically for licensed professional practice): Many state boards require licensed design professionals to organize under a PLLC rather than a standard LLC, since the PLLC form incorporates additional licensure-related ownership and naming requirements specific to regulated professions. This preserves liability protection for business debts similar to a standard LLC while satisfying the board's professional practice rules. Option A is wrong because general partnerships carry no such automatic permission, C is wrong because nonprofit status does not remove licensure requirements, and D is wrong because sole proprietorships face the same entity-level licensure scrutiny as any other structure.
  11. The correct answer is C (S-corporation election allows income to pass through to shareholders, avoiding corporate-level double taxation): Electing S-corporation tax status lets a professional corporation's income pass through directly to shareholders' personal tax returns rather than being taxed once at the corporate level and again when distributed as dividends. This tax treatment is a common reason firms organized as PCs elect S-status. Option A confuses tax election with insurance obligations, B incorrectly claims the election changes the underlying entity type, and D is wrong because S-election is a federal tax choice unrelated to state licensure ownership rules.
  12. The correct answer is D (Partners are generally shielded from personal liability for a co-partner's individual negligence): The defining feature of a limited liability partnership is that it protects each partner from personal liability arising from another partner's individual malpractice, while a partner generally remains personally liable for their own negligence. This distinguishes an LLP from a general partnership's joint and several liability exposure. Option A describes general partnership liability rather than LLP protection, B incorrectly extends the shield to the negligent partner's own claim, and C wrongly assumes liability is shared equally regardless of the LLP structure's purpose.
  13. The correct answer is A (A sole proprietorship, paired with a robust individual professional liability insurance policy): For a solo practitioner prioritizing the simplest formation process and willing to rely on insurance rather than entity structure for protection, a sole proprietorship paired with strong liability coverage matches these stated priorities better than a more complex entity. This approach trades entity-level liability protection for speed and simplicity. Option B requires additional owners the architect does not have, C involves more formation complexity than desired, and D cannot legally form with a single practitioner.
  14. The correct answer is C (Studio-based structure, in which self-sufficient teams manage projects and client relationships together): A studio-based structure organizes staff into self-sufficient teams that carry a project and its client relationship from initial contact through completion, which builds continuity and mentorship within each team. Option A describes functional grouping by specialty rather than integrated teams, B describes a dual-reporting structure for shared resource allocation, and D describes an unrelated flat hierarchy concept that does not match the described team model.
  15. The correct answers are A, B, and C: A matrix structure has staff reporting to both a discipline or department leader and a project manager, which increases flexibility in allocating specialists across concurrent projects but also creates a genuine risk of authority conflict between the two reporting lines. Option D is wrong because matrix staffing is explicitly designed around flexible, changing assignments rather than permanent placement, E is wrong because project managers remain essential under a matrix model, and F is an overgeneralization not supported by how client relationships actually function in practice.
  16. The correct answer is B (Current assets expected to be converted to cash within one year): prepaid expenses and accounts receivable are both expected to convert to cash or be used up within the normal operating cycle, so they are classified as current rather than fixed or intangible assets. Long-term liabilities and contributed capital appear on the opposite side of the balance sheet entirely. Intangible assets refer to non-physical assets like goodwill, not receivables or prepayments.
  17. The correct answer is A (150%): dividing indirect overhead expenses of $450,000 by direct labor expense of $300,000 yields 1.50, or 150%. This figure tells the principal how much overhead cost the firm carries for every dollar of direct labor and is a key input into setting billing multiples. Reversing the numerator and denominator, as in 67%, produces a different and less useful ratio.
  18. The correct answer is C (4.0): dividing net operating revenue of $1,200,000 by direct labor expense of $300,000 yields 4.0. The net multiplier shows how many dollars of revenue the firm generates for every dollar spent on direct labor, and it is the figure principals compare against the break-even multiplier to judge profitability. The other values do not result from correctly applying this formula to the given figures.
  19. The correct answer is D (The firm is operating below break-even and losing money on most work): a net multiplier of 2.1 that falls below a break-even multiplier of 2.4 means revenue is not covering direct labor plus overhead on those contracts. This signals a real financial problem, not strong profitability, and overhead being too low would actually lower the break-even multiplier rather than raise it. Lowering billing rates further would only widen the gap.
  20. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: rent, unbillable proposal writing time, marketing salaries, and insurance premiums are all costs the firm incurs regardless of a specific client project and are therefore indirect overhead. Direct labor billed to a client project and outside consultant fees passed through on a contract are direct project costs, not overhead, because they are traceable to a specific billable engagement.
  21. The correct answer is A (80%): dividing 1,600 billable hours by 2,000 total hours worked yields 0.80, or 80%. The utilization ratio measures the proportion of an employee's time spent on billable client work versus unbillable administrative or marketing time, and it directly affects the firm's revenue-generating capacity. The other values do not follow from correctly applying the formula.
  22. The correct answer is D (Income statement): the income statement, sometimes called a profit and loss statement, reports revenue and expenses over a defined period and arrives at net profit or loss. The balance sheet instead reports assets, liabilities, and equity at a single point in time, while the cash flow statement tracks cash movement rather than accrual-based revenue and expense recognition.
  23. The correct answer is D (Financing activities): drawing on a line of credit is a borrowing transaction that changes the firm's debt obligations, which places it in the financing activities section of the cash flow statement. Operating activities cover day-to-day revenue and expense cash flows, and investing activities cover the purchase or sale of long-term assets, neither of which describes a credit line draw.
  24. The correct answer is C (The firm's collection procedures and the creditworthiness of its clients): a sharp jump in severely past-due receivables signals a breakdown in how invoices are followed up on, or that certain clients are becoming financial risks, and both should be reviewed before more work is committed. This trend does not itself indicate the overhead rate or direct labor levels need adjustment, and it has no direct connection to the insurance deductible.
  25. The correct answer is A (The firm absorbs the cost if actual hours worked exceed the hours assumed when setting the fee): under a fixed fee, the firm is paid the agreed amount regardless of how many hours it actually takes, so any underestimate of effort reduces the firm's profit. This is the opposite of hourly billing, where the client bears the cost of additional hours. A fixed fee does not guarantee profit and does not shift risk to the client.
  26. The correct answer is D ($120): multiplying the $40 direct personnel expense by the 3.0 multiple yields a $120 hourly billing rate. This multiple-of-DPE method is a common hourly compensation structure that builds overhead recovery and profit into the rate charged for each hour of staff time. The other values do not result from applying the stated multiple correctly.
  27. The correct answer is B (Hourly billing at a multiple of direct personnel expense): when scope and the number of design iterations cannot be reliably predicted, hourly billing allows the firm's compensation to grow along with the actual effort required, protecting its profitability. A fixed lump sum, a construction-cost-based fee fixed upfront, or a stipulated sum without additional services allowances all expose the firm to loss if the project requires more effort than anticipated.
  28. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: how well the scope is defined, the level of risk inherent in the project type, the firm's cash flow and staffing needs, and its historical performance on similar projects are all legitimate financial and operational considerations in choosing a billing method. The client's preferred font and the proposal cover color have no bearing on compensation structure decisions.
  29. The correct answer is C ($160,000): multiplying the $2,000,000 construction cost estimate by the 8% fee rate yields $160,000. Under a percentage-of-construction-cost fee structure, the architect's compensation is tied directly to the project's construction cost, which means the fee should be recalculated as cost estimates are refined throughout the project. The other values do not correctly apply the 8% rate to the given cost.
  30. The correct answer is D (Professional liability, or errors and omissions, insurance): this coverage responds specifically to claims that a firm's professional judgment, design, or advice was negligent or deficient. General liability instead covers bodily injury and property damage unrelated to professional services, workers' compensation covers employee injuries, and commercial property covers the firm's own physical assets.
  31. The correct answer is A (General liability insurance): general liability covers third-party bodily injury and property damage claims that arise from the firm's premises or general business operations, which is exactly the situation of a visitor injured in the office. Professional liability applies to design errors, workers' compensation applies to employee injuries, and cyber liability applies to data breaches, none of which fit this scenario.
  32. The correct answer is B (Cyber liability insurance): cyber liability policies are specifically written to respond to data breaches, including notification costs, forensic investigation, and related third-party claims arising from a network intrusion. General liability and workers' compensation do not address data security incidents, and professional liability covers design negligence rather than cyber events.
  33. The correct answer is C (Workers' compensation insurance): workers' compensation is a statutorily required coverage in most states that pays for medical treatment and lost wages when an employee is injured in the course of employment, regardless of fault. Professional liability, property, and general liability insurance are not designed to cover an employer's own employees' workplace injuries.
  34. The correct answer is D (Commercial property insurance): commercial property insurance covers direct physical loss or damage to a firm's own owned or leased business property, such as furniture, equipment, and documents, which matches this water damage scenario exactly. Professional liability, workers' compensation, and general liability all address different types of third-party or employee claims rather than the firm's own property losses.
  35. The correct answers are A, C, and E: ransomware, a data-exposing phishing attack, and a hacked email account used to redirect a payment are all classic cyber incidents that a cyber liability policy is designed to address. A slip-and-fall injury falls under general liability, a design error causing a structural defect falls under professional liability, and a vehicle accident falls under auto liability coverage, none of which are cyber events.
  36. The correct answer is A (The firm could be required to cover losses caused by others, not just its own negligence): broad indemnification language tied only to claims arising out of the project, rather than claims caused by the architect's negligence, can obligate the firm to cover losses it did not actually cause. This is a significant and often uninsurable risk that firms typically negotiate to narrow. The clause does not prevent lawsuits, void insurance, or have no practical effect.
  37. The correct answer is B (The delivery methods most frequently requested by the firm's target clients and project types): a firm's decision about which delivery methods to actively market should be grounded in real market demand from the clients and sectors it serves. Choosing based on documentation burden, avoiding required insurance, or eliminating consultant coordination are not legitimate or even feasible business drivers, since every delivery method still requires appropriate insurance and consultant coordination.
  38. The correct answer is B (It allows constructability input and cost estimating to shape the design earlier): CMAR brings the construction manager onto the team during design so pricing and constructability feedback can influence decisions before documents are finalized. It does not eliminate construction observation duties, does not remove the need for a contractor, and does not guarantee a higher design fee.
  39. The correct answer is C (Adopting relational, multi-party agreements and shared risk and reward structures): IPD depends on multi-party contracts that align incentives among owner, architect, and builder, which is a fundamental firm-wide operational shift from traditional two-party agreements. Reducing design phases, assigning a single junior staffer, and eliminating the BIM model all contradict how IPD actually functions.
  40. The correct answer is A (Whether the firm can secure a construction license or a licensed contractor partner): design-build typically requires either the design-build entity to hold or partner with a licensed contractor, so leadership must confirm this capability exists before pursuing the work. Marketing materials, code review obligations, and client retention are secondary concerns that do not address the core licensing and delivery structure requirement.
  41. The correct answers are A, B, and D: adding CMAR as a specialization genuinely requires staff training in early estimating and GMP review, confirmation that insurance covers the added coordination role, and updated fee structures and contract templates. Local jurisdictions do not ban delivery methods outright, future client programs will vary rather than stay identical, and CMAR does not eliminate the architect's construction contract administration duties.
  42. The correct answer is A (Developing standard risk-allocation guidance and training on both design-build team roles): building firm-wide competency for either design-build role requires clear guidance on risk allocation and cross-training so any team can perform either function. Requiring a contractor's license for every project manager, refusing non-lead roles, or dropping quality control all limit rather than build firm-wide capability.
  43. The correct answer is D (Contractor pricing and constructability feedback usually arrive after bidding, not during design): in design-bid-build, the contractor is typically selected only after construction documents are complete, so the firm should set expectations that early-phase constructability input from a contractor is generally unavailable. The other options misstate the actual contractual sequence and legal requirements of this delivery method.
  44. The correct answer is C (Strengthen the firm's standard scope-definition and basis-of-design documentation practices): a pattern of scope-related claims specific to design-build points to a documentation and expectation-setting gap that a firm-wide standard can address. Abandoning design-build entirely or ignoring the pattern fails to fix the root cause, and reducing documentation would make the problem worse.
  45. The correct answers are B and D: services required because of significant owner-requested changes after approval, and preparing measured drawings of existing conditions not originally scoped, both fall outside typical basic services and warrant additional compensation. Preparing the standard design phases, reviewing submittals for conformance, attending routine meetings, and preparing a single standard bid package are all part of typical basic services.
  46. The correct answer is A (Publish a firm-wide scope-of-services matrix keyed to the firm's standard agreement): a documented, consistent matrix tied to the firm's standard contract gives every studio a shared reference for what counts as basic versus additional services. Letting each office define scope independently perpetuates the inconsistency, and removing the additional services clause or performing unbilled work both create financial and legal risk.
  47. The correct answer is C (Preparing the standard set of construction documents for the agreed project scope): producing the agreed construction documents is a core basic service under most standard agreements. Litigation testimony, cost estimating beyond general conformance review, and redesign due to a fundamental program change are all typically treated as additional services.
  48. The correct answer is B (Providing existing-conditions verification through as-built measurement and documentation): measuring and documenting existing conditions on a renovation is commonly treated as a supplemental or additional service because it exceeds typical new-construction basic services. Coordinating standard disciplines, the typical number of site visits, and a standard schedule of values are part of ordinary basic services.
  49. The correct answers are A, B, and D: requiring written documentation before starting extra work, training staff to recognize scope creep, and building a standard change-in-services form all give the firm concrete tools to control scope. Automatically treating verbal requests as contracted scope or refusing to discuss scope boundaries would make scope creep worse, not better.
  50. The correct answer is C (That sustainability certification administration is always excluded from basic design services): LEED or similar certification administration is generally treated as a distinct additional service because it involves specialized documentation and coordination beyond typical basic design services. Excluding it from every agreement, folding it automatically into every base fee, or tying payment to certification outcome are all inconsistent with standard practice.
  51. The correct answer is C (Add post-occupancy evaluation as a defined additional service with its own fee structure): formally defining post-occupancy evaluation with its own scope and fee gives every studio a consistent way to respond to client interest. Absorbing the cost into every base fee, declining all requests, or leaving pricing to informal improvisation each create inconsistency or lost revenue.
  52. The correct answer is A (The firm's project profitability erodes as unbilled scope expansion becomes normalized): consistently performing extra work without billing for it directly reduces project margins and, over time, normalizes the practice across the firm. Insurance premiums, client assumptions about services offered, and licensing authority are not directly affected by this specific billing pattern.
  53. The correct answer is B (Add a detail-oriented job captain experienced in wall assemblies to lead the section drawings): matching a team member's specific technical strength, wall assembly detailing, to the phase's actual deliverable need is the core resource management skill of aligning staff capability with workload. Reassigning the project architect away from oversight duties removes needed quality control at a critical phase. The principal-in-charge is generally not billed to production tasks and doing so misallocates the firm's most expensive resource. Delaying the deliverable does not solve the staffing mismatch and risks the schedule.
  54. The correct answer is C (Meet with both project managers to reprioritize tasks and rebalance the staff member's weekly hours): resource leveling across concurrent projects requires the responsible managers to coordinate directly and redistribute the shared resource's time based on relative priority and deadline proximity. Relying on sustained overtime across two full assignments invites errors and burnout rather than solving the underlying conflict. Replacing an experienced staff member on both projects discards institutional knowledge and adds onboarding time the schedule cannot absorb. Escalating to clients before attempting internal rebalancing is premature and undermines confidence in the firm's project management.
  55. The correct answer is A (Coordinating daily production activities and directing drafting staff on a specific set of drawings): the job captain typically manages the day to day production of a defined drawing set, coordinating the work of production staff and resolving detail level issues as they arise. Maintaining the overall client relationship is generally a responsibility of the principal-in-charge or project manager, not the job captain. Setting firm-wide production standards is a practice management function that sits above any single project team. Approving fee structure and additional services authorization is a contractual responsibility held by the principal-in-charge or project manager, not the job captain.
  56. The correct answers are A, B, and D: a new job captain joining mid-project needs a walkthrough of the current drawing set and open coordination issues, introductions to the subconsultant team and client contact, and a clear picture of the remaining fee budget, schedule, and hours to work within. Reassigning the job captain to redesign the structural system exceeds the role's scope and duplicates completed engineering work. Rewriting the entire specifications manual alone is an unnecessary and inefficient use of onboarding time. An exit interview with the departed team member is not a standard onboarding step and may not even be available.
  57. The correct answer is D (Whether existing staff have time to properly train and quality control their work): temporary contract staff unfamiliar with the project's standards and coordination status still need oversight and quality control from the existing team, and if no one can provide that supervision the added resource may not actually speed delivery or could introduce errors. Rate comparisons matter for budget but do not determine whether the resource will function effectively on this schedule. Insurance broker input on liability is a secondary consideration, not the primary driver of a staffing decision on a specific project. Remote work preference is a logistical detail, not the central factor in whether augmentation will help meet the deadline.
  58. The correct answer is A (Request a current staffing plan showing hours assigned to the project each week): asking for a current staffing plan gives the project manager concrete evidence of whether the subconsultant has assigned sufficient hours and personnel to recover the schedule, which is the necessary first step before deciding on further action. Terminating the agreement outright without first diagnosing the cause of the delay is a drastic step that risks a larger schedule disruption. Absorbing structural scope in-house is generally not feasible or appropriate given licensing and liability boundaries between disciplines. Waiting until construction documents are due allows the staffing shortfall to compound and removes any opportunity for early correction.
  59. The correct answer is B (The project architect shifts focus from design concept development to technical coordination oversight): as a project moves from schematic design into construction documents, the project architect's role typically transitions from developing the design concept to overseeing technical coordination among disciplines and maintaining design intent through detailed documentation. The project architect generally continues attending key client meetings through construction documents, not stopping entirely. Daily drafting tasks remain with production staff and the job captain, not the project architect. The project architect does not step away from the project, since design intent still requires their ongoing oversight through later phases.
  60. The correct answer is C (Investigate the cause of the overrun and adjust staffing or scope before it worsens): identifying whether the overrun stems from scope creep, inefficient staffing, or unanticipated coordination issues lets the project manager make a timely correction, such as reallocating staff or discussing scope with the client, before the phase budget is further exceeded. Continuing to monitor passively ignores a clear early warning sign that the phase is trending over budget. Instructing staff to log hours inaccurately falsifies project records and undermines the firm's ability to manage the project or bill appropriately. Waiting until the phase is finished to raise the issue removes any chance to correct course while it still matters.
  61. The correct answer is C (Construction Documents, which finalizes drawings and specifications ready for permitting and bidding): this phase follows Design Development and produces the detailed technical drawings and specifications a contractor needs to bid and a jurisdiction needs to issue a permit. Schematic Design instead explores massing and feasibility, and Design Development refines materials and systems before final documentation begins. Bidding or Negotiation is a separate phase that follows Construction Documents rather than producing them.
  62. The correct answer is A (Total float, the scheduling slack available before a task affects the project finish): total float measures how much a task can slip without pushing back the overall project completion date. Lag time is a fixed waiting period inserted between two dependent activities rather than a measure of slack. Duration and milestone describe a task's length and a zero-duration event respectively, neither of which describes available scheduling flexibility.
  63. The correct answer is D (To define how information, decisions, and documents move between the owner, architect, and consultants): a communication protocol establishes reporting lines, points of contact, and how requests for information and design decisions are routed on a specific project. It does not calculate fee, determine insurance coverage, or assign copyright, all of which are addressed elsewhere in the owner-architect agreement. Establishing this protocol at kickoff prevents confusion once the project is underway.
  64. The correct answers are A, B, and D: identifying genuine predecessor relationships, assigning realistic durations based on actual scope, and linking tasks only where a real dependency exists all produce an accurate, usable work plan for this specific project. Scheduling every task on the same date ignores real sequencing needs, ignoring consultant input risks missing dependencies only they can identify, and copying an unrelated project's structure without review can introduce dependencies that do not actually apply here. Building dependencies deliberately, rather than by shortcut, keeps the schedule a reliable planning tool.
  65. The correct answer is B (Finish-to-start, the most common dependency where a successor waits for its predecessor to finish): this is the most frequently used relationship in a project schedule, where one task cannot begin until its predecessor is fully complete. Start-to-start and finish-to-finish tie tasks together by their start or finish dates rather than requiring full completion first. A lag dependency adds a waiting period but does not by itself describe this sequential relationship.
  66. The correct answer is C (The owner-architect agreement, which sets the phase-by-phase fee breakdown for the project): AIA agreements such as B101 typically allocate the architect's compensation across Schematic Design, Design Development, Construction Documents, and Construction Contract Administration within the agreement itself. The general conditions govern the owner-contractor relationship, not the architect's fee. A geotechnical report and a certificate of substantial completion address site conditions and construction closeout, not fee allocation.
  67. The correct answer is D (Whether the project team is burning fee faster or slower than the work performed): comparing fee spent to percent complete reveals whether the team is on pace, ahead, or falling behind budget relative to actual progress on that project. Contractor bidding, owner finish selections, and permit status are separate tracking items unrelated to this specific earned value comparison. Catching a burn rate imbalance early lets the project manager address its cause before it grows.
  68. The correct answer is A (Each task has zero float, so any delay to it delays the overall finish): tasks on the critical path have no scheduling slack, so any slip in their start or finish date directly extends the project's overall completion date. Staffing assignments, owner approval requirements, and which phase a task occurs in do not define critical path status. Identifying these zero-float tasks lets the project manager prioritize monitoring where delay risk is highest.
  69. The correct answer is B (A project-specific scope definition describing what this project's services will and will not include): before sequencing tasks, the team needs a clear statement of this project's specific deliverables and boundaries, distinct from the firm's general basic and additional services policy. A marketing brochure, a construction cost estimate, and an insurance certificate do not define project scope. Establishing scope first prevents tasks from being sequenced around undefined or assumed deliverables.
  70. The correct answer is D (Assess how the delay affects downstream tasks and the project's critical path): before reacting, the project manager needs to understand whether the engineer's two-week request actually threatens the schedule's critical path or can be absorbed by available float. Notifying the owner that the whole project is behind, removing the engineer, or unilaterally raising the fee are premature actions that skip this necessary analysis. Understanding the true impact first supports an appropriate, proportionate response.
  71. The correct answer is C (The steel order date slips, threatening the project's substantial completion milestone): because structural steel requires a long fabrication lead time and must be ordered before Construction Documents finish, a three-week design delay directly threatens the order date and the downstream substantial completion milestone. An insurance premium increase, a forced change of general contractor, and repeating Schematic Design are not direct consequences of this specific delay. Long lead items like structural steel make schedule float on their design tasks especially critical.
  72. The correct answers are A, B, and E: evaluating whether critical path tasks can be compressed, promptly communicating the delay and its impact to the owner, and updating the work plan to reflect revised dates are all appropriate responses to a critical path delay. Reassigning the task to a consultant simply because they have more float does not address the actual cause of delay, assuming a delay has no effect because fee is unchanged ignores real schedule risk, and waiting for the next scheduled meeting to report a critical path delay is too slow a response. Prompt, accurate action on critical path delays protects the project's finish date.
  73. The correct answer is A (Meetings should become more frequent, often weekly, to address active field conditions): Construction Contract Administration involves fast-moving field conditions, submittals, and requests for information that require more frequent coordination than earlier design phases. Stopping meetings, reducing them to quarterly, or moving entirely to email would leave the team unable to respond to active construction issues in a timely way. Meeting cadence should scale with the pace of decisions the project requires at each phase.
  74. The correct answer is B (Establishing a finish-to-start dependency between the equipment layout and finish selection tasks): since finish selections depend on knowing where ceiling-mounted equipment will be located, the layout task should finish before finish selection begins. Scheduling both tasks on the same day ignores the actual dependency between them, and removing the interior designer or postponing all selections does not solve the coordination need. Correctly sequencing dependent tasks like this avoids costly rework later in the project.
  75. The correct answer is D (Backward scheduling from a fixed milestone, working from the end date to today): when an external deadline like a fixed bid date cannot move, the project manager works backward from that date to determine when each preceding task must start and finish. Forward scheduling instead calculates a finish date from a known start date, which does not apply when the end date is already fixed. Fast tracking and resource leveling address different scheduling concerns, overlapping phases and balancing staff workload respectively.
  76. The correct answer is A (The team may be overspending fee relative to progress and needs a cause review): a burn rate well ahead of percent complete signals the team should investigate what is consuming disproportionate hours before the imbalance grows further. This comparison does not by itself confirm a missed invoice payment, a contractor's bid amount, or a permit rejection, none of which are addressed by fee tracking. Catching this gap during Construction Documents allows corrective action before the phase concludes.
  77. The correct answer is C (Drawings and specifications establishing the required quality levels of materials and systems): under B101, Construction Documents phase basic services require the architect to prepare documents that establish quality levels of materials and systems for the project, building on approved Design Development Documents. Option A describes a closeout deliverable, not a Construction Documents phase requirement. Option B describes a cost management activity outside the architect's basic scope in most agreements. Option D describes Bidding or Negotiation phase services, a separate basic service phase.
  78. The correct answer is A (As an Additional Service requiring a separate agreement or amendment for compensation): evaluating alternate sites for potential relocation falls outside the Basic Services described in B101 Article 3 and constitutes an Additional Service under Article 4, since it was not part of the originally contemplated scope for the existing project site. Option B is incorrect because Design Development Basic Services address the approved project on its established site, not alternate site studies. Option C is incorrect because architects, not owner's consultants, typically perform this evaluation when requested. Option D is incorrect because Additional Services require compensation beyond the original agreement.
  79. The correct answer is D (Against the skill and care ordinarily exercised by architects in the same locality under similar circumstances): B101 defines the standard of care as the level of professional skill and care ordinarily provided by architects practicing in the same or a similar locality under the same or similar circumstances, not a guarantee of perfection. Option A is incorrect because the standard of care never guarantees error free work. Option B is incorrect because an owner imposed schedule alone does not redefine the contractual standard of care. Option C is incorrect because the benchmark is ordinary practicing architects, not the most elite firms nationally.
  80. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: a stipulated sum compensates the architect for a specifically defined scope, so a material scope change should be handled through a formal amendment that adjusts compensation accordingly, and performing significant added scope without that amendment risks the architect providing services without being paid. Option B is incorrect because a stipulated sum does not automatically expand to absorb owner directed scope changes. Option E is incorrect because stipulated sum agreements can be modified by mutual written amendment at any point in the project.
  81. The correct answer is B (Notify the owner the additional hearings constitute an Additional Service requiring compensation): when a project requires participation beyond the number of meetings or hearings anticipated in the original agreement, B101 treats the excess participation as an Additional Service requiring notice and compensation. Option A is incorrect because informal, after the fact discussion risks disputes over undocumented scope. Option C is incorrect because attending public hearings is a legitimate architect service when included or added to scope. Option D is incorrect because absorbing added scope without compensation is not required or advisable under the agreement.
  82. The correct answer is A (A written amendment signed by both the owner and the architect): B101 requires that modifications to the agreement, including changes to services such as the number of construction observation visits, be made through a written amendment executed by both parties. Option B is incorrect because a verbal agreement alone does not formally modify the written contract. Option C is incorrect because a unilateral email from one party does not bind the other party to a scope change. Option D is incorrect because an internal file note has no contractual effect on either party.
Case Study PJM_CON_C1

The architect is providing basic services under a B101 agreement for a 90,000 square foot mixed-use development consisting of ground floor retail and five floors of residential units above a structured parking podium. Midway through Construction Documents, the owner directs the architect to redesign the parking podium to add one additional level of below grade parking to meet a revised zoning requirement discovered during permitting. The architect's project manager estimates the redesign will require significant rework of structural, civil, and MEP documents already substantially complete, along with additional coordination meetings with the geotechnical consultant. The original B101 agreement was executed with a stipulated sum for basic services based on the original five story residential program and does not address subsurface parking expansion. The project manager must determine how to address the scope change, compensate the architect fairly, and keep the consultant team properly informed and compensated.

  1. The correct answer is C (Notify the owner in writing that the redesign is an Additional Service requiring an amendment): the added level of below grade parking was not part of the originally contemplated five story residential program, so it falls outside Basic Services and the architect must notify the owner and formalize compensation before proceeding. Option A is incorrect because beginning substantial rework without documented authorization risks uncompensated services. Option B is incorrect because delaying notice increases dispute risk and does not address consultant coordination needs. Option D is incorrect because the original stipulated sum was not priced to include this expanded scope.
  2. The correct answer is B (The redesign responds to a scope not contemplated in the original program and agreement): Additional Services are defined by work falling outside the scope the parties originally agreed to, and an added level of parking driven by a revised zoning requirement was not part of the original five story residential program. Option A is incorrect because timing within a phase does not by itself determine Basic versus Additional status. Option C is incorrect because using the same consultants does not change the classification of the underlying scope. Option D is incorrect because additional coordination meetings are a consequence of the scope change, not the defining characteristic.
  3. The correct answer is D (Negotiate a separate stipulated sum or hourly not to exceed fee specifically for the added scope): a defined additional scope like the parking podium redesign is typically priced separately, either as its own stipulated sum or an hourly not to exceed fee, and added to the agreement by amendment. Option A is incorrect because retroactively changing the fee without documenting scope invites disputes over what work it covers. Option B is incorrect because converting the entire remaining agreement is unnecessary when only a discrete portion of scope has changed. Option C is incorrect because architect compensation is governed by the owner architect agreement, not the contractor's general conditions markup.
  4. The correct answer is A (Amend the applicable C401 agreements to add the additional scope and compensation for each consultant): because C401 flows down terms from the prime B101 agreement, a scope and compensation change at the architect level should be mirrored through amendments to the affected consultant agreements. Option B is incorrect because informal direction does not bind the consultant to added compensation or documented scope. Option C is incorrect because terminating and reissuing agreements is unnecessary and disruptive when an amendment accomplishes the same result. Option D is incorrect because general professional competence does not obligate a consultant to perform scope beyond their contracted agreement.
  5. The correct answer is C (The architect may perform substantial services without an enforceable right to compensation): performing significant added scope before a written amendment is executed leaves the architect exposed, since the owner may later dispute that the work was authorized or that compensation was agreed upon. Option A is incorrect because standard of care obligations remain in effect regardless of whether an amendment has been signed. Option B is incorrect because delayed documentation does not prevent an owner from requesting further changes. Option D is incorrect because delayed paperwork does not itself trigger a termination for convenience.
  6. The correct answer is B (A specific description of the added scope, revised compensation, and any schedule impact): an effective amendment clearly documents what added scope is being authorized, how compensation changes, and how the schedule is affected, so both parties share a common understanding going forward. Option A is incorrect because a vague statement about the agreement remaining unchanged does not document the actual change being made. Option C is incorrect because the standard of care applies to all services performed and should never be waived. Option D is incorrect because a scope amendment supplements the existing agreement rather than replacing it entirely.
  7. The correct answer is D (Mediation followed by either binding arbitration or litigation, as selected by the parties): B101 requires claims to first go through mediation, after which the parties select in advance whether unresolved claims proceed to binding arbitration or litigation. Option A is incorrect because dispute resolution is not decided unilaterally by either the architect or the contractor. Option B is incorrect because licensing board appeals and informal negotiation are not the contractual dispute resolution path in B101. Option C is incorrect because B101 requires mediation as a mandatory first step before litigation or arbitration.
  8. The correct answer is A (The architect's fee may decrease proportionally since it is tied to the construction cost): under a percentage of the cost of the work method, compensation is calculated as a function of the actual construction cost, so a reduction in that cost through value engineering can reduce the calculated fee. Option B is incorrect because a percentage based fee is inherently variable with construction cost, not fixed. Option C is incorrect because a reduced construction cost does not by itself create an Additional Service. Option D is incorrect because the owner cannot unilaterally convert the compensation method without an amendment.
  9. The correct answer is D (Termination for convenience, entitling the architect to compensation for services performed plus termination expenses): when an owner terminates without alleging architect fault, B101 treats this as termination for convenience, and the architect is entitled to compensation for services performed to date along with termination related expenses. Option A is incorrect because there is no allegation of architect fault, so termination for cause does not apply. Option B is incorrect for the same reason and because B101 does not provide for doubled fees. Option C is incorrect because termination for convenience still requires the owner to compensate the architect for completed work and termination costs.
  10. The correct answer is B (Seven days written notice describing the substantial failure to allow the owner to cure): B101 generally requires the party alleging substantial failure to perform, including nonpayment, to provide seven days written notice before terminating the agreement for cause, giving the other party an opportunity to cure. Option A is incorrect because immediate termination without notice does not follow the contractual cure process. Option C is incorrect because B101 does not impose a lengthy cooling off period with no communication. Option D is incorrect because arbitration is a dispute resolution path for claims, not a prerequisite to sending a termination for cause notice.
  11. The correct answer is C (The dispute resolution method and standard of care applicable to the project): C401 is designed to bind the consultant to key terms of the prime agreement, including the dispute resolution method and the standard of care, so the consultant's obligations align with what the architect owes the owner. Option A is incorrect because internal staffing decisions are the consultant's own management matter, not a flowed down contract term. Option B is incorrect because marketing materials have no bearing on the contractual relationship. Option D is incorrect because unrelated business matters like an office lease are outside the scope of the project agreement.
  12. The correct answers are A, C, and D: for the risk allocation between the prime agreement and the consultant agreement to stay consistent, the standard of care, insurance requirements, and dispute resolution method should align so the architect is not exposed to obligations the consultant has not also assumed. Option B is incorrect because internal fee allocation is the consultant's own business matter. Options E and F are incorrect because software choice and font style are administrative preferences with no bearing on contractual risk allocation.
  13. The correct answer is A (A scope overlap that the architect should resolve to avoid duplicated or conflicting work): when two consultants' scope exhibits both claim responsibility for the same task, such as perimeter grading, the architect is responsible for coordinating scope so there is no overlap or conflicting design authority. Option B is incorrect because unintentional overlap creates confusion and risk rather than useful redundancy. Option C is incorrect because unresolved overlaps increase, not reduce, the architect's coordination burden. Option D is incorrect because duplicate invoicing for the same scope is not an appropriate contractual outcome.
  14. The correct answer is D (It helps ensure adequate coverage is available if the consultant's error contributes to a claim): matching professional liability insurance limits between the prime and consultant agreements helps ensure sufficient coverage exists to respond to a claim if the consultant's design work is found to have contributed to the deficiency. Option A is incorrect because insurance alignment does not create an automatic right of recovery for the owner against the consultant. Option B is incorrect because the architect retains its own liability exposure regardless of the consultant's coverage. Option C is incorrect because matching insurance does not shield the consultant from being named in a related claim.
  15. The correct answer is B (Explain that professional liability policies typically cannot add additional insureds and discuss alternatives): professional liability, or errors and omissions, insurance generally cannot add additional insureds the way commercial general liability policies can, so the project manager should clarify this distinction and discuss what coverage, such as certificates of insurance, may be appropriate instead. Option A is incorrect because it misstates how professional liability coverage typically functions. Option C is incorrect because declining to engage with a reasonable insurance question is not a constructive response. Option D is incorrect because the request concerns the architect's own coverage, not the contractor's.
  16. The correct answer is A (Coordinating consultant drawings and confirming deliverables meet the schedule for this project): this is the core day to day execution work of a project manager on a specific project, distinct from firm-wide business functions like insurance, billing rates, or hiring. Option B, C, and D all describe firm-wide practice management activities rather than the execution of an individual project. The PjM division tests the project manager's role in running one project, not managing the business of architecture.
  17. The correct answer is B (Convene the structural and mechanical consultants to resolve the clash before proceeding further): catching coordination conflicts early, before they carry forward into construction documents, is a core project execution responsibility and prevents costly rework later. Waiting until construction documents are complete or ignoring the issue until construction administration allows the conflict to become far more expensive to fix. Directing one consultant to act unilaterally risks a solution that does not actually resolve the underlying clash between disciplines.
  18. The correct answer is C (Meet with the interior designer to identify the cause and recover the float): investigating the root cause of a schedule slip and working with the responsible party to recover time is the most effective and proportionate response before the delay affects other disciplines. Extending the overall schedule immediately is premature without first understanding whether the delay can be recovered. Removing the consultant or reporting to the client without investigation are disproportionate reactions to a single discipline trending behind.
  19. The correct answers are A, C, and D: distributing an agenda in advance, assigning responsible parties and due dates to action items, and promptly issuing written minutes are all core practices that make OAC meetings productive and ensure follow-through on open issues. Running a meeting without structure, discussing confidential fee matters in an open forum, and allowing attendees to set the agenda informally all undermine the meeting's effectiveness and create unnecessary risk.
  20. The correct answer is D (Clear action items with responsible parties and target completion dates noted): minutes exist primarily to create an actionable record that the team can follow up on, so the most valuable content is a clear list of assigned actions and deadlines. A verbatim transcript is unnecessary and impractical, personal opinions have no place in a project record, and limiting the summary to only client-requested topics would omit important coordination items.
  21. The correct answer is A (Disclose the budget variance clearly along with proposed options for bringing it back in line): transparent reporting of both schedule and budget status, including any variances and proposed remedies, is essential to maintaining client trust and allowing timely decisions. Omitting or delaying disclosure of an unfavorable budget trend withholds information the client needs and increases risk if the variance grows before it is addressed.
  22. The correct answer is B (The specific unresolved coordination items that must close before the presentation): with a client presentation only three days away, the meeting agenda should prioritize the exact open items standing between the team and a successful presentation. General updates, unrelated firm standards discussions, and performance reviews are not appropriate uses of limited time before a hard deadline.
  23. The correct answer is D (Scope creep, which the project manager should track and document formally.): a pattern of small, individually minor additions that collectively expand the contracted scope is the classic definition of scope creep, and it must be tracked so its cumulative impact on fee and schedule can be assessed. It is not a routine adjustment requiring no documentation, nor is it a construction change directive or a value engineering exercise, both of which describe different situations.
  24. The correct answer is D (Document the change and pursue a formal amendment to the owner-architect agreement): adding an entire new building wing represents a material expansion of scope beyond the original contract and requires formal documentation and amendment before substantial design effort proceeds. Proceeding without informing the principal in charge or absorbing the work within the existing fee exposes the firm to significant uncompensated risk, and treating it as routine understates its actual scope impact.
  25. The correct answers are B and E: overlaying drawings from different disciplines to check for physical clashes and holding a scheduled interdisciplinary review before each milestone are the practices most directly designed to catch coordination conflicts before a submission reaches the client. Skipping internal review, assuming consultants have already coordinated independently, relying solely on each consultant's own quality control, and waiting until construction administration all allow conflicts to persist undetected until they become more costly to fix.
  26. The correct answer is A (Prioritize the permit submission project while maintaining light oversight of the other): allocating attention based on relative urgency, while not fully abandoning the lower-priority project, reflects effective prioritization across concurrent projects. Splitting attention exactly evenly ignores the real difference in urgency between the two projects, and either extreme of focusing entirely on one project while ignoring the other creates unnecessary risk.
  27. The correct answer is B (Contact the consultant directly to confirm status and identify any needed support): proactively following up when a deliverable appears at risk allows the project manager to identify problems and provide support before the deadline is missed. Assuming the deadline will be met without checking, removing the deliverable from the milestone unilaterally, or waiting until the deadline passes all allow a preventable delay to become a larger problem.
  28. The correct answer is C (To focus discussion on specific open items so attendees arrive prepared): a written agenda distributed in advance helps meeting participants prepare and keeps the discussion focused on the items that need resolution. It is not primarily a litigation record, a contractual formality without practical value, or purely an attendance tracking tool, even though it may incidentally serve secondary purposes.
  29. The correct answer is D (Reassess the schedule and communicate the revised timeline to the client promptly): when new information changes a key assumption in the master schedule, the project manager should update the schedule and inform the client as soon as possible so decisions can be made with accurate information. Ignoring the discrepancy, halting all work, or waiting for the client to notice all delay a necessary and proactive communication.
  30. The correct answer is A (The client may act on unverified information that has not been coordinated with the team): uncontrolled communication directly to the client, bypassing the project manager, creates risk because the client may rely on information that has not been vetted or coordinated with the rest of the design team. It does not automatically affect the consultant's fee, void insurance coverage, or genuinely improve the schedule, since uncoordinated information can create confusion rather than progress.
  31. The correct answer is B (To confirm the project team applied firm QC procedures to this project's actual current deliverables): a phase-end checkpoint exists to test the firm's QC standards against what was actually produced for this specific project, not generic policy. It catches project-specific errors before they carry forward into the next phase. Budget approval, billable hours, and contract terms are project management and financial matters, not the purpose of a technical QC checkpoint.
  32. The correct answer is D (Confirm that structural, mechanical, and architectural drawings show consistent floor to floor heights): a DD-phase QC checkpoint should verify technical coordination across disciplines on the actual project deliverables before they reach the client. Fee negotiation, contractor selection, and agreement amendments are administrative or contractual matters unrelated to a technical QC review of the drawings themselves.
  33. The correct answer is B (Orient the long axis east-west with most glazing facing south and shaded by properly sized overhangs): a south-facing orientation captures the low winter sun for passive heating while properly calculated overhangs block the higher summer sun angle to prevent overheating. Orienting the building north-south with evenly distributed glazing wastes the site's solar potential and increases unwanted heat gain and loss on the east and west facades. Concentrating glazing on the north facade minimizes useful solar gain and increases heating loads in a cold climate. West-facing glazing captures intense low-angle afternoon sun that is difficult to shade and causes summer overheating and glare.
  34. The correct answer is D (Measure wind speed and turbulence at the proposed mast height above nearby obstructions): turbines sited within the turbulent wake of trees or structures generate less energy and experience greater mechanical wear, so wind data must be gathered at the actual proposed height clear of obstructions. Matching turbine output to demand is a system-sizing task that does not address whether the physical location can capture usable wind. Prioritizing wiring distance ignores the resource assessment that determines whether the turbine will perform at all. Minimizing visual impact from the road is a valid secondary consideration but does not substitute for confirming the site has adequate, unobstructed wind.
  35. The correct answer is A (Shift and reshape the building footprint to preserve the grove as a site amenity): mature trees represent a natural landscape feature and an environmental opportunity that adds value, shade, and stormwater benefit, so the design should respond to the site rather than clear it. Removing the grove and replanting sacrifices decades of canopy growth and the neighborhood value the trees already provide. Leaving the footprint decision to the contractor abandons the architect's responsibility to resolve site opportunities during design. Reducing the grove to a few specimen trees still eliminates most of the shading and habitat benefit the grove offers.
  36. The correct answer is C (Specify high-albedo paving and roofing materials along with shade trees on site): light-colored, reflective surfaces and added tree canopy lower surface and air temperatures, directly countering the heat absorbed and re-radiated by dark pavement in the surrounding context. A dark, low-albedo roof would add to the same heat-absorbing condition the project is trying to mitigate. Expanding paved parking increases the impervious, heat-absorbing surface area rather than reducing it. Maximizing reflected glare onto the street creates a separate nuisance and does not lower ambient temperature.
  37. The correct answer is B (Step the upper floors back and align the base with the neighboring roof and setback lines): stepping the massing back preserves the perceived scale of the historic street wall at the pedestrian level while still allowing the building to achieve its full program above. Building to the maximum height with a uniform street wall would tower over the surrounding two- and three-story homes and disrupt the established rhythm. Using an unrelated style ignores the socio-cultural context the client asked the design to respond to. Placing the tallest volume at the street edge maximizes the contrast the neighborhood context is meant to guide against.
  38. The correct answers are A, C, and D: the 100-year floodplain is the area with a 1 percent annual chance of flooding rather than a literal once-per-century event, structures within the Special Flood Hazard Area typically must have their lowest floor elevated above the base flood elevation, and the base flood elevation is defined as the expected water surface elevation for that 1 percent annual chance event. The 500-year floodplain still carries a measurable annual flood risk and can still warrant floodproofing or elevation depending on jurisdiction, so it is inaccurate to say it carries no risk at all. A Special Flood Hazard Area designation does not by itself prohibit development, though it does trigger additional design and permitting requirements. Flood zone maps are periodically revised as hydrology and development patterns change, so a zone designation is not fixed once issued.
  39. The correct answer is A (An added margin of height above the base flood elevation to reduce future flood risk): freeboard is the extra elevation built above the base flood elevation to provide a buffer against wave action, future map revisions, and modeling uncertainty. It is not a reduction in the base flood elevation, which is a fixed hydrologic value for the site. Freeboard is a vertical elevation requirement, not a horizontal setback from the floodway. While adequate freeboard can influence insurance rating, the requirement itself is a construction elevation standard rather than a direct premium credit.
  40. The correct answer is D (Use a deep foundation system or a stiffened slab designed for expansive soil movement): expansive clays require foundations engineered to resist or accommodate the swelling and shrinking cycle, such as drilled piers or a properly reinforced stiffened slab. A standard shallow footing sized only for dead load does not account for the uplift and settlement forces expansive soils generate. Disregarding the geotechnical report ignores documented site conditions that directly affect structural performance. Simply enlarging the footprint spreads the load but does not resolve the differential movement caused by moisture-driven soil expansion.
  41. The correct answer is C (Commission subsurface investigation to map voids before finalizing the building location): karst terrain can conceal underground voids and active sinkhole formation that surface observation alone cannot reveal, so subsurface investigation is needed to confirm whether the proposed location is stable enough to build on. Treating the depressions as purely cosmetic ignores a documented indicator of subsurface dissolution. Limestone bedrock in karst regions is often unpredictable and can have reduced, not enhanced, bearing capacity where voids exist. Filling the depressions without further testing masks a warning sign rather than addressing the underlying hazard.
  42. The correct answer is B (Install a sub-slab depressurization system with vent piping routed to the roof): a sub-slab depressurization system draws radon gas from beneath the foundation and vents it above the roofline before it can enter occupied space, which is the standard passive mitigation approach for elevated radon sites. Increasing ceiling height does not address the source of soil gas entry. Extra foundation wall insulation does not seal the pathways radon typically uses, such as slab cracks and penetrations. A vapor barrier under the roofing membrane has no effect on gas entering from the soil beneath the building.
  43. The correct answer is A (Engage a certified lead abatement contractor to safely remove or encapsulate the material): lead-based paint disturbance is regulated and requires trained, certified personnel to remove or encapsulate the hazard using containment and safe work practices before construction proceeds in that area. Simply covering deteriorating paint with new drywall does not eliminate the hazard and can trap loose material behind the new surface. Sanding lead paint without containment releases hazardous lead dust into the occupied environment. Leaving the paint undisturbed only defers the problem and does not resolve the deteriorating condition that already exists.
  44. The correct answer is D (Commission a Phase II Environmental Site Assessment to test soil and groundwater conditions): a Phase I assessment identifies potential recognized environmental conditions but does not sample or test, so a Phase II assessment with soil and groundwater testing is needed to confirm whether contamination is actually present and at what concentration. Proceeding directly to construction documents ignores a documented condition that could affect site feasibility and cost. Dismissing the finding because solvent use was common elsewhere does not confirm the condition on this specific property. A verbal assurance from the seller is not a substitute for independent environmental testing.
  45. The correct answers are B, C, and E: excavating and properly disposing of contaminated soil, installing a vapor barrier and sub-slab venting to block vapor intrusion, and applying institutional controls such as a deed restriction are all recognized approaches to managing brownfield contamination depending on the site's risk profile. Capping the site with landscaping alone, without testing or engineering controls, does not manage vapor intrusion or contact risk. An insurance policy from a previous owner does not certify soil conditions and is not a substitute for environmental testing. Assuming a commercial, non-residential use eliminates the need for remediation ignores vapor intrusion and worker exposure risks that exist regardless of occupancy type.
  46. The correct answer is B (Group A-2, assembly, for a space used for the consumption of food or drink): a restaurant with concentrated, fixed-seat or table dining is the defining example of the A-2 assembly group under the IBC. Business classification applies only when food service is incidental to an office or similar primary use, not a restaurant's primary function. Mercantile applies to retail sales floors, not dining areas, and A-3 covers assembly uses without food or drink consumption, such as a lecture hall. The occupant load and seating arrangement here point squarely to A-2.
  47. The correct answer is D (Type II-B, noncombustible construction with no required fire-resistance rating): Type II construction requires the structural frame, floors, and roof to be built of noncombustible materials, and the B subtype specifically carries no minimum fire-resistance rating for those elements. Light-frame wood construction describes combustible Type V assemblies, which do not match a noncombustible frame. A protected noncombustible exterior with combustible interior elements describes Type III, and heavy timber describes Type IV, neither of which matches a fully noncombustible, unrated structural frame.
  48. The correct answer is A (provide an automatic sprinkler system throughout the building per NFPA 13, which qualifies for a height increase): the IBC grants a defined height and story increase for buildings equipped with a required automatic sprinkler system throughout, tied to the actual construction type and occupancy. Lowering occupant load below an alarm threshold does not change the tabulated height and area limits. Reclassifying the occupancy without changing the building's actual use misrepresents the project and is not a legitimate code strategy. Adding a stair improves egress capacity but has no direct effect on the allowable height calculation.
  49. The correct answer is C (1:12, or 8.33 percent): the ADA Standards for Accessible Design set 1:12 as the maximum running slope permitted for a new ramp run along an accessible route, with a maximum rise of 30 inches between landings. A slope of 1:8 is far too steep and would be treated as a curb ramp condition at best, not a standard accessible ramp. A slope of 1:10 also exceeds the permitted maximum. A slope of 1:15 is gentler than required and would be acceptable but is not the stated maximum threshold the standard establishes.
  50. The correct answers are A, C, D, and F: an initial code analysis establishes the governing occupancy classification and construction type, the resulting allowable height and area, required fire-resistance ratings and exit separation, and applicable accessibility requirements. A client's preferred finish palette is a design and material selection decision, not a code parameter. The choice of structural engineer is a project team staffing decision unrelated to code analysis. These two items are business and design preferences rather than regulatory findings that shape the building's governing parameters.
  51. The correct answer is B (International Energy Conservation Code, or an equivalent adopted state energy code): envelope insulation values, air leakage rates, and fenestration U-factors are all regulated by the energy code applicable in the project's jurisdiction. The structural provisions of the building code govern loads and framing, not thermal performance. The fire code governs fire prevention and life safety operations, not envelope energy performance. The mechanical code's ventilation provisions address airflow and equipment sizing, not the envelope assembly values described here.
  52. The correct answer is A (LEED certification does not substitute for mandatory code compliance, though many LEED credits build on code minimums): LEED is a voluntary third-party rating system, and the project must still independently demonstrate compliance with the jurisdiction's mandatory energy code through the normal permitting process. Registering for LEED does not automatically satisfy the energy code, and no building official is required to waive code review because a project pursues certification. LEED's energy credits are in fact built directly on top of code baseline requirements, so the two are closely related rather than largely different.
  53. The correct answer is D (the most restrictive applicable requirements of all occupancies involved apply to the entire building): when occupancies are not separated by required fire-resistance-rated construction, the IBC requires the entire building to comply with the most restrictive applicable provisions among the occupancies present.
