A transfer structure is the standard way to carry the upper grid's loads down to the widely spaced columns below. The height cap and sustainability target matter elsewhere in the project, but neither one forces this specific structural transition.
  2. The correct answer is C (Main ducts will likely need rerouting around the girders, reducing available ceiling height at that floor): a transfer girder occupies more depth in the ceiling plenum than a typical flat plate slab, so ductwork sharing that same plenum has less clear space to pass through. The mechanical engineer typically has to route around or below the deepest structural members, which can lower the finished ceiling at that floor unless the team finds another path. This is a direct example of how a structural configuration decision constrains a system that must physically share the same space. Neither the rooftop unit's location nor the retail span is affected by this particular conflict.
  3. The correct answer is A (Steel transfer trusses are typically deeper than an equivalent concrete girder, requiring more ceiling height): swapping the structural system changes the physical dimensions of the member carrying the load, not just its material. A steel truss spanning the same distance as the concrete girder is generally deeper, which would further squeeze the second-floor ceiling plenum where the ductwork already has to route. This illustrates why evaluating a change in one system requires checking its impact on every other system sharing that same space, not just on cost or schedule. The lease terms, floodplain survey, and facade datum lines are unrelated to this particular structural substitution.
  4. The correct answer is D (It reduced ground-floor area otherwise available for the column-free retail space): moving the mechanical room out of the basement and above the base flood elevation means it has to occupy space somewhere else in the building, most practically near grade, which competes with the retail tenant's usable floor area. This is a direct tradeoff created by resolving an environmental site condition, the floodplain, within the building's overall configuration. The transfer girders, the office floor-to-floor height, and the retail span are each driven by other factors in the scenario, not by this equipment relocation.
  5. The correct answer is C (The structural depth of the long-span roof system needed to clear the court): a ninety-foot column-free span requires a deep long-span structural system such as a truss or girder, and that depth directly sets the minimum floor-to-floor height for the gym portion of the addition. The classroom wing's twenty-five-foot bay does not need to match the gym's configuration since the two portions of the building can use different structural systems. Parking ratios and locker room plumbing fixture counts affect other parts of the project but do not drive the roof structure's required depth.
  6. The correct answer is A (Dividing the building with a rated fire wall into two separate allowable areas connected by a bridge): building codes allow a project to exceed the single-fire-area limit by separating the building into distinct fire areas using a rated wall, effectively creating two separately measured allowable areas without changing the construction type. This is a code-driven configuration strategy that directly resolves the area limitation. Parking counts, stormwater rate, and roof height are unrelated to how allowable building area is calculated under the code.
  7. The correct answer is D (The upper-floor massing and street-facing cornice alignment of the new addition): historic preservation guidelines of this kind typically govern how a new addition reads from the street, including setbacks and cornice alignment with neighboring historic buildings, so this requirement primarily shapes the addition's massing and facade configuration. It does not dictate the parking ramp design, mechanical system selection, or the fire suppression system's water pressure, which are governed by other code and engineering requirements.
  8. The correct answer is B (Configuring a narrow floor plate oriented to admit daylight and cross-ventilation): a narrower floor plate, properly oriented to the site's solar and wind data, allows daylight to reach further into the space and enables cross-ventilation, both of which directly support a daylighting and natural ventilation strategy. Maximizing below-grade parking, changing the fire suppression system's water demand, and enlarging the elevator core do not affect how daylight or airflow move through the occupied floors.
  9. The correct answer is C (It would free up floor area by reducing the size of the mechanical shafts): variable refrigerant flow systems use smaller refrigerant piping instead of large ductwork risers, so switching to this system generally allows the vertical shafts serving the building to shrink, freeing up leasable or usable floor area on each level. This system change would not by itself require additional floor-to-floor height, remove the need for a fire separation between tenants, or force the exterior walls to become curtain wall.
  10. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: relocating structural columns can conflict with mechanical shafts and ductwork already routed above, so the team must confirm there is no clash. The new grid also needs to still support the planned office tenant layout, since column locations constrain interior partitions. Plumbing risers and electrical conduit typically align with column locations in a coordinated design, so those need rechecking as well, and any structural change of this scale can affect cost and schedule and should be evaluated before it is accepted. Paint color and irrigation zone labeling are cosmetic details that do not depend on column locations and would not require this kind of coordination review.
  11. The correct answer is A (Confirm the roof structure can carry the additional saturated soil and vegetation load, resizing framing if needed): an extensive green roof adds substantial dead load from saturated growing medium and vegetation well beyond a standard membrane roof, so the existing framing must be checked and likely resized to safely carry that added weight. Relocating the electrical service, reducing accessible parking, and increasing plumbing fixture counts have no bearing on whether the roof structure can support the new load.
  12. The correct answer is B (It requires a penthouse enclosure and additional roof height above the elevator shaft): a machine room located at the top of a traction elevator shaft needs an enclosed space above the roof line to house the hoisting equipment, which adds a penthouse volume and additional height to the roof configuration. This equipment choice does not eliminate the need for a perimeter parapet, reduce drainage capacity requirements, or change the building's fire separation rating.
  13. The correct answer is D (By introducing a seismic separation joint dividing the wings into distinct structures): an irregular plan with wings of significantly different height and mass can behave unpredictably during an earthquake if left as one continuous structure, so engineers commonly introduce a seismic joint to separate it into simpler, more predictable structural units. This response affects the building's structural configuration directly, unlike relocating the building entirely, splitting the mechanical system into zones, or doubling the fire suppression water supply, none of which resolve the underlying seismic irregularity.
  14. The correct answer is C (It sets aside full mechanical floors at outrigger levels, interrupting the typical residential plan): an outrigger system ties the core to perimeter columns at specific levels, and those levels are typically dedicated to mechanical equipment rather than residential units because the outrigger walls and connections occupy the floor. This interrupts the otherwise repetitive residential floor plate at those levels. The system does not require parking below every floor, relocate the entrance away from wind, or remove the need for a rated exit stair enclosure.
  15. The correct answer is B (The tendon locations must be located and confirmed clear of the proposed penetration): post-tensioned slabs contain tensioned cables that can be seriously compromised if cut, so any new penetration must be checked against as-built tendon locations, typically using a scanning method, before drilling proceeds. Occupant load calculations, curtain wall water testing, and elevator machine room verification are unrelated to the safety of cutting a new slab penetration.
  16. The correct answer is A (The column grid should be arranged to keep bays clear of the planned exhaust shaft locations): laboratory fume hood exhaust risers require dedicated vertical shafts running the full height of the building, and the structural bay layout should be planned so columns do not conflict with those shaft locations. Widening the grid to eliminate interior shafts is not practical for numerous fume hood risers, and parking ratio or exterior facade material choices do not resolve where the exhaust shafts can be located.
  17. The correct answer is D (Align chase locations across floors as closely as possible, even where fixture layouts differ): stacking plumbing and mechanical chases as consistently as possible across dissimilar floor types minimizes offsets, reduces cost, and simplifies coordination, even when the exact fixture layout varies by use. Routing each floor's chases independently, forcing every chase to the exterior wall, or consolidating all fixtures on one floor would each create an inefficient or impractical vertical distribution system for a mixed-use tower of this height.
  18. The correct answer is C (That the existing floor-to-floor height and structural capacity can accommodate the raised floor and added loads): converting office space to a data center adds a raised access floor, which reduces usable clear height, and introduces server rack point loads well beyond typical office live loads, so both floor-to-floor height and structural capacity must be verified before proceeding. The building's sprinkler type, elevator cab size for furniture delivery, and parking garage truck clearance are not directly implicated by this particular conversion.
  19. The correct answer is B (It limits how much of the timber structure can remain visually exposed within the building): when a code allows exposed mass timber only up to a certain height and requires a portion of the structure to remain encapsulated, the design must plan which structural elements can stay exposed and which must be covered, directly shaping the interior architectural expression. This requirement does not affect the parking ratio, remove the need for a rated exit stair enclosure, or require plumbing fixtures to move to the perimeter.
  20. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: the roof structure must be verified to carry the cooling tower's operating weight along with seismic loading, and the equipment's noise output needs adequate acoustic separation from patient rooms and other sensitive spaces nearby. Make-up water piping and electrical connections must be routed to the unit, and the team also needs to plan screening and maintenance access clearances for the life of the equipment. Exterior signage color and lobby furniture style have no bearing on where mechanical equipment can be safely and functionally located on the roof.
  21. The correct answer is A (It would require additional floor-to-floor height to accommodate the raised floor plenum): underfloor air distribution relies on a raised access floor to create a supply air plenum beneath the occupied floor, and that plenum adds height that a conventional overhead system does not require in the same way, so the building's floor-to-floor height typically needs to increase to accommodate it. The system would not eliminate the height allowance entirely, reduce it below the overhead system's requirement, or leave floor-to-floor height unaffected.
  22. The correct answer is B (Whether diagonal braces would obstruct the large ground-floor window openings.): a braced frame relies on diagonal members that can physically conflict with large storefront openings, while a moment frame achieves lateral resistance through rigid beam-column connections without diagonal bracing, making it more compatible with an open storefront facade. Exterior masonry color, accessible parking counts, and ground-floor plumbing fixture counts do not influence which lateral system is compatible with the storefront glazing.
  23. The correct answer is C (It requires a separate bracing frame to stabilize the retained facade independent of the new structure): a retained historic facade with new construction behind it, sometimes called facadism, is not self-supporting once the original building behind it is demolished, so an independent bracing frame must hold the facade in place and tie it back to the new structure as it is built. This requirement does not mean the mechanical system must match the original building, that no new structural system is needed, or that the new building's height must match the old building exactly.
  24. The correct answer is D (Whether to include occupied space or critical equipment below grade at all.): a high water table combined with a mapped floodplain significantly increases the risk and cost of below-grade construction, including waterproofing challenges and flood exposure, so the team should seriously reconsider whether occupied space or critical equipment belongs below grade in this configuration. Roof slope, elevator quantity, and exterior veneer material are not directly driven by groundwater or floodplain conditions.
  25. The correct answer is A (Required setbacks from property lines, noise impact on occupied space, and fuel code requirements): emergency generator and fuel storage placement is governed by required setback distances, applicable fuel storage code provisions, and the need to keep generator noise away from occupied and sensitive spaces, all of which directly determine where this equipment can be sited in the building configuration. Lobby finish colors, parking space counts, and landscaping species selection do not factor into this life-safety equipment placement decision.
  26. The correct answer is C (By reserving a dedicated shaft location that stacks through every floor above the restaurant.): a Type I kitchen exhaust system requires a continuous, code-compliant shaft from the hood to the rooftop discharge point, so the building's configuration on every floor above the restaurant must reserve a consistent shaft location for that duct to pass through. This requirement does not eliminate the need for a rated shaft enclosure, allow the ground-floor grid to widen, or affect the site's accessible parking count.
Case Study PDD_IBM_C1

An architect is renovating a six-story mixed-use building that will add a new rooftop mechanical penthouse to house replacement air handling units, extend the existing elevator to serve a new roof-level mechanical room, and replace the original single-glazed curtain wall with a high-performance unitized system. During design development, the structural engineer proposes new steel roof framing with beam depths of 24 inches to support the added mechanical loads, but the mechanical engineer's ductwork layout for the penthouse air handlers requires a continuous 18-inch clear plenum below the new framing to maintain code-required duct sizing without reducing the mechanical room's finished ceiling height below the minimum required for equipment servicing. The curtain wall subcontractor's shop drawings show anchor brackets bolted directly to the existing spandrel beams at each floor line, coinciding with the location where the mechanical engineer wants to route a new refrigerant piping riser between the mechanical room and a fifth-floor tenant space. The new elevator shaft extension penetrates the fifth-floor slab, which is rated for one-hour fire resistance separating the tenant space below from mechanical spaces above. The architect must coordinate these systems to keep the project on schedule while resolving the conflicts before construction documents are issued.

  1. The correct answer is B (Convene the structural and mechanical engineers to jointly explore beam or duct revisions): when two engineering systems conflict on space within a tight zone, the architect's coordinating role is to bring the disciplines together so the beam depth and duct depth can be jointly resolved rather than unilaterally decided by one party. Reducing duct size to increase air velocity could push noise and static pressure beyond acceptable limits without engineering review. Raising the parapet does not address a conflict located within the roof framing depth itself. Approving the framing as designed and forcing a ceiling drop was already stated to violate the minimum required servicing clearance, so it is not an available option.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reroute the riser to run between anchor points, verified with the structural engineer): rerouting the piping to run between the curtain wall's fixed anchor points, coordinated with the structural engineer, resolves the spatial conflict without disturbing the anchorage that transfers wind and gravity loads from the curtain wall into the spandrel beam. Relocating or resizing the anchor brackets without structural engineering input risks compromising the curtain wall's load path and warranty. Asking the mechanical engineer to find a completely different vertical path ignores a workable in-place solution and could create new conflicts elsewhere in the building.
  3. The correct answer is A (A fire-rated shaft enclosure with tested, listed firestopping at the slab penetration): a penetration through a fire-resistance-rated floor assembly must be protected with a through-penetration firestop system that has been tested and listed to maintain the assembly's rating, contained within a properly rated shaft enclosure. A smoke damper alone addresses only smoke movement in an air distribution duct, not a structural penetration for an elevator shaft. Standard gypsum board without a tested firestop assembly does not restore the required fire-resistance rating at the opening. An open shaft relying only on the elevator door assembly leaves the slab penetration itself unprotected against fire and smoke passage.
  4. The correct answer is C (Mount the units on vibration isolators set on a structurally isolated curb): mounting rooftop air handling units on vibration isolators supported by a structurally isolated curb interrupts the direct structure-borne path that would otherwise transmit mechanical vibration and noise down through the roof framing into the occupied space below. Increasing ceiling height in the mechanical room affects airborne sound within that room, not structure-borne transmission through the building frame. A thicker roof deck alone adds mass but does not decouple the vibration source from the structure. Relocating the units to another roof corner still places them above occupied space and does not solve the isolation problem at its source.
  5. The correct answer is B (Specify a 4-inch drain, sized per the table for the full roof area): the primary drain must handle the full 6,000 square foot roof area at the required rainfall intensity, and only the 4-inch drain's rated capacity of 6,428 square feet exceeds that area, making it the minimum size that satisfies the sizing table. A 3-inch drain rated for only 3,214 square feet is undersized for this roof area and would not meet code. This drain choice does not eliminate the separate code requirement for a secondary overflow drain or scupper, so omitting that system would be a code violation. Splitting the load across two smaller drains changes the design approach entirely and was not what the table-based sizing called for here.
  6. The correct answer is A (Confirm the connection type with the structural engineer before finalizing the detail): because the structural engineer's specified connection type governs the actual load path and behavior of the joint, the architect must confirm the intended connection type before finalizing a detail that shows a different configuration, since a shear connection and a moment connection have fundamentally different design assumptions and detailing requirements. Issuing the detail as drawn assumes architectural details take precedence over structural design intent, which is not the case for load-resisting connections. Deleting the continuity plates without confirming intent could either strand unneeded material or remove a required load path. Changing the connection type unilaterally in the structural drawings oversteps the architect's role and bypasses the engineer of record's design responsibility.
  7. The correct answer is D (The 40-amp breaker, the next standard size above the calculated load): branch circuit overcurrent protection must be sized at or above the calculated demand load, and the next standard breaker size above 38 amps is 40 amps, which protects the circuit without unnecessary oversizing. A breaker rated below the 38-amp calculated load would nuisance-trip or fail to protect the circuit adequately. A far larger breaker provides more capacity than needed and would require a correspondingly oversized, more expensive conductor. An intermediate size is not justified simply by conductor insulation temperature rating when a smaller standard size already exceeds the calculated demand.
  8. The correct answer is C (Require a resilient channel assembly with staggered studs and added mass): achieving the specified STC 55 rating from a wall assembly that only tested at STC 40 typically requires added mass and a decoupled structure, such as staggered or resilient-channel-mounted studs with additional gypsum layers, to interrupt the direct sound transmission path. Accepting the undersized assembly ignores a real, measurable performance gap between what was specified and what was built. Adding carpet to the conference room floor addresses impact noise on a floor above, not airborne sound transmission through a demising wall. Lowering the specified rating to match the deficient construction abandons the acoustic performance the project required instead of correcting the assembly.
  9. The correct answer is C (9 heads, rounding the coverage area calculation up): dividing the 2,000 square foot area by the maximum 225 square foot coverage per head yields 8.89, and sprinkler head counts must be rounded up to ensure no portion of the floor area exceeds the maximum coverage per head, resulting in a minimum of 9 heads. Estimating from approximate square footage without the actual coverage calculation risks undercounting the heads needed. Rounding the calculation down would leave a portion of the room outside any head's maximum coverage area. Adding a head for an adjacent corridor is a separate design consideration unrelated to the open office area's own coverage requirement.
  10. The correct answers are A, B, and C: when obstructions are discovered during sprinkler layout review, the design team should add supplemental heads where beam obstructions exceed the standard's permitted dimensions, relocate any heads whose discharge pattern is blocked by a fixture, and recalculate spacing to confirm the coverage area per head still complies with the standard. Leaving the layout unchanged ignores obstructions that were specifically identified as a problem. Removing the light fixtures is an unnecessary and often impractical response when relocating the affected heads would resolve the obstruction. Increasing water pressure does not address a physical obstruction blocking a sprinkler's discharge pattern.
  11. The correct answer is A (Confirm with the structural engineer whether the openings affect lateral capacity): because shear walls resist lateral seismic and wind loads by acting as continuous vertical diaphragms, any new opening through them must be evaluated by the structural engineer to confirm the wall retains adequate lateral capacity after the shaft penetrates five floor levels. Proceeding on the assumption that shear walls only resist gravity loads is factually incorrect and could compromise the building's seismic performance. Widening the shaft for construction convenience without structural review only increases the risk to the wall's lateral capacity. Assuming the opening is acceptable simply because it matches the elevator cab size ignores the actual structural analysis required for a load-bearing wall.
  12. The correct answer is D (Coordinate a factory-prepped mullion or surface raceway with the manufacturer): coordinating a factory-prepared mullion with an internal raceway, or an approved surface raceway, with the curtain wall manufacturer provides a code-compliant, weathertight path for the low-voltage conduit without compromising the sealed extrusion's performance. Field-drilling through the mullion extrusions risks breaching the curtain wall's air and water seals and voiding the manufacturer's warranty. Relocating the readers away from the curtain wall may not be feasible given the vestibule's door configuration and defeats the original design intent. Using wireless readers exclusively eliminates a stated project requirement for low-voltage conduit rather than resolving the actual routing conflict.
  13. The correct answer is B (Have the structural engineer verify allowable penetration locations and added load capacity): because the structural engineer's original design did not account for cable tray loads or the specific penetration locations through the joist webbing, the architect must have the structural engineer verify which penetration locations are acceptable and confirm the joists have adequate capacity for the added load. Approving the routing without that verification assumes the loads are negligible without any engineering confirmation. Rerouting everything below the ceiling in exposed raceways abandons the concealed installation without first exploring whether the original approach can be validated. Leaving the routing decision entirely to the low-voltage contractor bypasses the structural review that a penetration through load-bearing framing requires.
  14. The correct answers are A, C, and D: maintaining continuity of the building envelope's barriers at this transition requires lapping the air barrier membrane over the sill flashing leg, sealing the water-resistive barrier to the flashing with a compatible sealant, and installing a transition membrane compatible with both the wall and window systems to bridge the two assemblies. Terminating the vapor retarder at the rough opening without connecting it to the adjacent system leaves a gap in the assembly's control layers. Relying on the curtain wall gasket alone ignores the separate air barrier continuity requirement across the transition. Leaving a gap between barriers for anticipated movement defeats the purpose of a continuous air and water control layer and should instead be addressed with a flexible, compatible transition detail.
  15. The correct answer is A (Approximately 17,300 lumens, dividing by both the CU and LLF factors): dividing the product of footcandles and area, 9,000, by both the coefficient of utilization of 0.65 and the light loss factor of 0.80 yields approximately 17,300 lumens, correctly accounting for both light losses in the fixture and room and the depreciation of lamp output over time. Multiplying only footcandles by area ignores both correction factors and understates the lumens actually required. Dividing by only the coefficient of utilization while omitting the light loss factor understates the required output by ignoring lamp depreciation. Dividing by only the light loss factor while omitting the coefficient of utilization ignores how efficiently the fixture and room surfaces deliver light to the work plane.
  16. The correct answer is D (The 1.25-inch pipe, since it is rated above the calculated demand): the 1.25-inch pipe's rated capacity of 44 fixture units exceeds the branch's calculated demand of 42 fixture units, meeting the code's supply pipe sizing table at the required pressure range. A smaller pipe rated for only 27 fixture units falls well short of the 42 fixture unit demand and would be undersized. Doubling a smaller pipe in parallel is not how the code's sizing table is applied and introduces unnecessary complexity. Specifying the correctly sized pipe now and downsizing later based on unconfirmed future counts abandons the calculated demand that has already been established.
  17. The correct answer is A (Whether the concrete's bearing strength requires a steel bearing plate to distribute the load): a steel beam bearing directly on concrete without a bearing plate can create highly concentrated stress that may exceed the concrete's bearing capacity, so the structural engineer must verify whether a steel bearing plate is needed to distribute the load over a larger area. The beam's paint finish and the canopy's overall length are architectural or aesthetic considerations that do not address the structural bearing condition. Welding the beam directly to the concrete surface is not a valid structural connection method for transferring a bearing load into concrete.
  18. The correct answer is D (A slip-type mullion connection sized for the full 2 inches of movement): a curtain wall crossing a building seismic movement joint must use a slip-type mullion connection sized to accommodate the full 2 inches of anticipated lateral movement without restraining the two building sections from moving independently. A rigid mullion splice bolted solidly across the joint would defeat the purpose of the movement joint and could damage the curtain wall during a seismic event. A standard butt-glazed joint sealed only with sealant is not designed to accommodate structural movement of this magnitude. A fixed anchor connection tying both building sections together directly contradicts the intent of an isolation joint meant to allow independent movement.
  19. The correct answer is D (Coordinate raising the parapet height or adding a cant to meet the minimum): because the roofing manufacturer's minimum flashing height is a warranty and performance requirement, the architect must coordinate either raising the parapet or adding a cant or curb to achieve the required 8-inch minimum above the finished roof surface. Terminating the flashing at only 6 inches falls short of the manufacturer's minimum and risks water infiltration and warranty voidance. Substituting a different product to avoid the height requirement changes the specified roofing system without addressing the underlying detailing issue. Adding a cricket redirects water flow but does not by itself satisfy the minimum flashing height requirement at the parapet termination.
  20. The correct answer is B (Whether the racking system's specific anchor point loads are within capacity): while the array's distributed dead load of 4 pounds per square foot falls within the roof's 5 pound per square foot allowance, the racking system's concentrated anchor point loads must still be checked individually since point loads can locally exceed capacity even when the average distributed load does not. Confirming nothing further ignores the point load condition, which is a separate structural question from the average distributed load. Relocating panels to avoid roof penetrations does not address whether the anchor points as designed are within structural capacity. Whether the roofing warranty covers photovoltaic installation is a contractual matter separate from the structural load verification the architect must confirm.
  21. The correct answer is C (Add sound-absorptive finishes and panels to reduce the reverberation time): adding sound-absorptive finishes and panels reduces the amount of sound energy reflecting off hard surfaces, shortening reverberation time and improving speech intelligibility for spoken-word use while still supporting the room's music function. Increasing ceiling height would tend to lengthen reverberation time further, worsening the intelligibility problem the consultant identified. Adding more hard, reflective surfaces increases reflected sound energy and would make the excessive reverberation time worse, not better. Removing the amplified sound system does not address the room's underlying acoustic problem and abandons a stated use of the space.
  22. The correct answer is A (Coordinate the standpipe riser location and space allocation with the fire protection engineer): the architect's role is to coordinate the physical space, riser location, and clearances the standpipe system needs within the building layout, working with the fire protection engineer who performs the hydraulic design. Performing the hydraulic calculation directly falls outside the architect's scope and responsibility, since that engineering analysis belongs to the fire protection engineer of record. Reducing the required flow rate to simplify pipe sizing would compromise the life-safety system's performance and is not a decision the architect can make unilaterally. Approving the calculated values without coordinating space and access needs skips the architect's actual coordination responsibility on this system.
  23. The correct answer is B (Have the structural engineer evaluate options to gain the additional pit depth): because the required pit depth exceeds the clearance the structural foundation design currently allows, the architect must have the structural engineer evaluate options, such as a deeper foundation or alternate framing, to gain the additional pit depth the escalator manufacturer requires. Selecting a different escalator model with a shallower rise does not address the pit depth requirement, which is largely independent of the escalator's overall rise. Instructing the contractor to excavate into the existing footing without structural evaluation could compromise the foundation's load-carrying capacity. Reducing the incline angle does not eliminate the manufacturer's required pit depth beneath the lower landing.
  24. The correct answers are A, B, and C: resolving a conflict between a structural beam and a mechanical duct at the same ceiling elevation appropriately involves convening the structural and mechanical engineers to jointly evaluate routing options, investigating whether a shallower beam is structurally feasible at that bay, and investigating whether splitting the duct into two smaller parallel ducts could resolve the clash. Directing the contractor to notch the beam flange without engineering review risks compromising the beam's structural capacity. Lowering only the corridor ceiling without evaluating routing alternatives may create a headroom problem without actually resolving the underlying systems conflict. Reducing the duct's cross-sectional area without recalculating required airflow could leave the mechanical system unable to deliver its design airflow.
  25. The correct answer is D (Require a detail sealing the membrane around each dowel penetration): a reinforcing dowel penetrating a below-grade waterproofing membrane creates a potential leak path that must be detailed with a sealing method around each penetration to maintain the membrane's watertight integrity. Accepting the drawings without a penetration detail ignores a real risk of water infiltration at each column location. Removing the waterproofing membrane eliminates the protection the below-grade structure needs rather than detailing the specific penetrations. Relocating all columns to avoid the membrane is impractical and unnecessary when a properly detailed penetration seal is achievable.
  26. The correct answer is B (The mullion surface stays above the dew point, so condensation is not expected here): because the mullion's calculated interior surface temperature of 48 degrees Fahrenheit is above the space's 45 degree dew point, the surface stays warmer than the temperature at which moisture in the air would condense, so condensation is not expected at this detail. The claim that the mullion will condense contradicts the numbers given, since its surface temperature is above, not below, the dew point. Dismissing the mullion's thermal performance as irrelevant ignores that framing surface temperature is exactly what determines condensation risk at a curtain wall. Requiring a thermal break based on a general assumption about metal frames ignores the specific calculated result showing this detail already performs above the dew point.
  27. The correct answer is C (The structural engineer must verify or reinforce the roof for the added load): because the proposed green roof's saturated dead load of 35 pounds per square foot far exceeds the existing structure's 20 pound per square foot capacity with no reserve allowance, the structural engineer must verify the existing framing or design reinforcement before the system can be approved. Proceeding on the assumption that green roofs are inherently lightweight ignores the specific saturated load value calculated for this system. Reducing soil depth without structural verification is an unconfirmed assumption rather than an engineered solution to the load deficit. Limiting the green roof to the perimeter zone does not by itself resolve the load capacity shortfall without structural analysis.
  28. The correct answer is D (Revise the tie spacing to 16 inches on center, per the manufacturer's data): because the manufacturer's engineering data for the veneer's actual weight requires wall ties at 16 inches on center horizontally, the architect must revise the standard detail's 24-inch spacing to match that engineered requirement rather than defaulting to the office standard. Keeping the wider spacing because it matches a standard detail ignores the manufacturer's specific engineering data for this veneer's weight. Adjusting only the vertical spacing does not compensate for a horizontal spacing that has been determined to be inadequate. Switching to a lighter veneer material changes the design without confirming the tie spacing issue has actually been resolved for the veneer being used.
  29. The correct answer is B (Coordinate a larger operable sash or additional openings to meet the assumed free area): because the energy model relied on a 4 percent net free area assumption that the specified window's 2.5 percent operable sash does not meet, the architect must coordinate a larger operable sash or additional openings to achieve the ventilation area the model assumed. Proceeding with the window as specified ignores a real gap between the design assumption and the actual product performance. Removing the natural ventilation strategy from the energy model without further coordination abandons a design strategy rather than resolving the shortfall. Increasing mechanical capacity to compensate changes the building's energy strategy rather than addressing the specific window performance gap identified.
  30. The correct answer is C (Facilitate a coordination meeting to reroute the pipe around the beam): facilitating a coordination meeting between the design team allows the fire protection engineer and structural engineer to jointly determine how to reroute the sprinkler main around the beam without compromising either system's performance. Having the fire protection engineer resize the pipe until it physically fits could reduce the pipe's hydraulic capacity below what the sprinkler system's design requires. Instructing the contractor to field-cut the beam risks compromising the beam's structural capacity without any engineering review. Lowering the entire ceiling plenum to gain clearance is a far more costly and disruptive solution than first attempting to reroute the pipe.
  31. The correct answer is A (Relocate the camera along the ceiling grid to an unobstructed mounting point): relocating the camera along the ceiling grid to an unobstructed mounting point preserves the required corridor sightline while keeping the camera within a standard, accessible ceiling location. Removing the structural beam is not a feasible solution to a camera placement conflict, since the beam is a load-bearing element. Lowering the camera below the beam only partially clears the sightline, which still risks the camera being obstructed or aimed incorrectly. Accepting a partial obstruction is not appropriate when the specific purpose of this camera was to monitor the full length of the corridor.
  32. The correct answer is A (That the structural engineer has verified the truss for these specific point loads): because the truss was designed for uniformly distributed loading, the structural engineer must verify that the truss can safely carry the concentrated point loads the air handling units introduce at mid-span panel points before equipment locations are finalized. Selecting units with the lowest available weight does not by itself confirm the truss has been verified for the specific point loads at these locations. Confirming the roofing membrane is rated for foot traffic addresses a different concern unrelated to the truss's structural capacity for point loads. Whether the units are painted to match the roof surface is a purely aesthetic matter unrelated to the structural coordination required here.
  33. The correct answer is D (The slope is compliant, since 1/8 inch per foot is permitted for pipes 3 inches and larger.): because the code permits a shallower 1/8 inch per foot slope specifically for drainage pipes 3 inches and larger in diameter, the 4-inch branch sloped at 1/8 inch per foot meets the applicable minimum slope requirement. Calling the slope deficient applies the general minimum meant for pipes 2 1/2 inches and smaller to a pipe that falls under the separate, larger-pipe provision. Reducing the pipe size to justify a shallower slope would actually make the pipe noncompliant, since smaller pipes require the steeper 1/4 inch per foot minimum. Shortening the run length has no bearing on which slope minimum applies to a given pipe diameter.
  34. The correct answers are A, B, and C: improving impact sound isolation above a hard-surface floor effectively involves adding a resilient underlayment beneath the flooring, suspending the ceiling below on resilient channel decoupled from the joists, and adding mineral wool insulation within the floor and ceiling cavity, each of which reduces structure-borne impact sound transmission. Increasing the flooring's gloss level is a purely visual characteristic with no effect on impact sound isolation. Attaching the ceiling gypsum board rigidly to every joist creates a direct structural path that transmits impact sound rather than isolating it. Painting the ceiling a lighter color has no acoustic effect and does not address the impact insulation class rating.
Case Study PDD_CD_C1

You are the project architect for a six-story mixed-use building undergoing significant renovation, transitioning from design development into the construction documents phase. The project team includes a civil engineer documenting site drainage and utility connections, and a curtain wall consultant detailing the ground-floor storefront and upper-floor punched window systems. During a coordination meeting, the civil engineer flags that the proposed loading dock grading conflicts with an existing municipal stormwater easement along the site's east property line, requiring the paving and drainage plan to be revised and cross-referenced on the architectural site plan. Separately, the owner requests a value engineering change to substitute the specified aluminum storefront system with a lower-cost alternative to control budget, a change that arrives after the storefront details have already been drawn and issued for a 75 percent CD review set. The construction manager, engaged during this phase for preconstruction services, asks your office to confirm which drawings require revision clouds and delta numbers for the reissued set, and whether the substitution affects the fire-rated assembly at the storefront head condition shown in the wall section detail. Your office must decide how to document the revised site drainage coordination, update the affected detail drawings and specifications consistently, and determine whether the storefront substitution triggers additional structural or fire-protection review before the CD set is finalized and issued for permit.

  1. The correct answer is B (Cross-reference the civil engineer's revised drainage plan on the site plan and coordinate matching spot elevations): the architect is responsible for coordinating the site plan with the civil engineer's drainage design rather than duplicating or omitting it. Cross-referencing keeps each discipline's drawings as the record document for its own scope while ensuring the spot elevations and grading agree between the architectural and civil sets. Redrawing the civil engineer's work directly onto the architectural sheet creates a duplicate record that can fall out of sync during future revisions. Leaving the conflict unresolved or omitting the loading dock grading entirely would leave a known coordination issue undocumented going into permit.
  2. The correct answer is C (Cloud and number only the sheets containing actual changes, and list each delta with a brief description): standard practice for a reissued set marks only the sheets that actually changed with revision clouds and sequential delta numbers, each paired with a short description of the change. This lets the contractor, reviewers, and construction manager quickly identify what is new without re-reviewing an entire set that is mostly unchanged. Clouding every sheet regardless of content defeats the purpose of the clouding system and forces an unnecessary full re-review. Relying on clouds only at the cover sheet, or skipping clouds and using a transmittal memo instead, fails to flag the specific drawing locations where content actually changed.
  3. The correct answer is D (Verify the substitute system has tested fire-rated head assembly data equivalent to the original detail): because the original storefront head detail was drawn specifically to satisfy a required fire rating, any substitution must be checked against tested assembly data showing the replacement performs equivalently at that same condition. Fire-rated assemblies are only code compliant when installed as tested, so a different framing profile or glazing system cannot be assumed to carry the same rating without documentation. Approving the substitution without further review risks issuing a detail that cannot actually achieve the required rating in the field. Deferring the decision to field adjustment or deleting the rating requirement from the specification would abandon a code-driven requirement rather than resolve it.
  4. The correct answer is A (The changes require re-detailing previously completed drawings and re-verifying a fire-rated assembly after a review milestone): additional services are typically triggered when changes require substantive rework of drawings that were already completed and issued, particularly when that rework includes re-verifying a code-related performance requirement such as a fire rating. Re-detailing after a review milestone represents real added effort beyond the scope of basic services already performed for that phase. A single clarification email, a general scheduling question, or a construction manager's routine attendance at a coordination meeting are all part of ordinary coordination and do not by themselves indicate scope beyond what basic services already covers.
  5. The correct answer is C (Show representative connection types and design intent, referencing the delegated engineer's detailed connection design for final sizing): when connection design is delegated to the contractor's engineer of record, the architect of record's drawings should establish design intent and representative connection types while referencing the delegated engineer's documents for final engineered sizing. This preserves a clear division of responsibility consistent with the delegated design arrangement while still communicating the architect's overall design. Showing full design-level detail duplicates work that belongs to the delegated engineer, while omitting structural information or showing only unlabeled locations fails to communicate the design intent the architect still must convey.
  6. The correct answer is A (Update every affected detail, plan callout, and specification reference consistently so the set reflects current rainscreen data): when a specified system's manufacturer data changes, every drawing and specification reference tied to that system must be updated together so the issued set remains internally consistent. Leaving plan callouts or specification sections unrevised while only the wall section changes creates a document set where different sheets describe different products, inviting bidding confusion and field conflicts. Waiting until permit issuance to make a known correction, or updating only a single document, only postpones a needed fix rather than resolving it.
  7. The correct answer is D (Update the finish schedule, floor plan, and specification section together to reflect the final selected flooring material): a final material selection must be reflected consistently across the finish schedule, the plan, and the specifications so bidders receive one coordinated description of the actual product rather than conflicting information across documents. A verbal note at a pre-bid meeting is not a reliable substitute for the drawn and written record bidders rely on to price the work. Referencing the material only by name in the specifications, or issuing a late memo after bidding closes, leaves the drawings themselves out of step with what was actually selected.
  8. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: stormwater drainage connections, utility service entrance locations, vehicular and pedestrian circulation, and site grading with finished floor elevations are all site-level conditions that depend on coordination between the architect, civil engineer, and landscape architect, so each belongs on the architectural site plan. Interior gypsum board specifications and millwork paint color are building interior finish decisions with no bearing on site coordination or the civil and landscape disciplines. Site plan coordination specifically concerns features that cross the property, building entry, and utility interfaces, not interior material selections. Limiting cross-disciplinary review to true site conditions keeps the coordination effort focused on what actually requires input from civil and landscape consultants.
  9. The correct answer is B (Show the coordinated service entrance location on the site plan and reference it consistently on the electrical drawings): coordinating a utility service entrance location between the utility company and the civil engineer only has value if that agreed location is then shown on the site plan and referenced consistently on the electrical drawings. Showing the location on only one discipline's drawings risks a mismatch between where the utility company expects the service and where the electrical engineer designs the building connection. Leaving the location undetermined, or describing it only in a narrative, fails to give the contractor a coordinated, buildable location for site work.
  10. The correct answer is A (Show the accessible route with clear striping, signage, and grade information where it crosses the drive aisle): where an accessible route crosses a vehicular drive aisle, the construction documents must clearly show striping, signage, and grade information so the crossing is safe, code compliant, and clearly buildable. Showing only the parking space without the connecting route leaves the actual path across the drive aisle undocumented. A specification note without a drawn location, or reliance on the general contractor to determine the route once site work begins, does not give bidders or the building department the specific information needed to evaluate compliance before construction.
  11. The correct answer is C (Coordinate directly with the civil engineer to confirm a single top-of-wall elevation and revise both documents): when two disciplines show a differing elevation at the same physical condition, the architect must coordinate directly with the civil engineer to agree on a single correct top-of-wall elevation and then revise both sets of documents to match. Assuming the discrepancy will self-correct during construction risks the contractor building to whichever number they reference first. Changing only the architectural sheet, or deleting the dimension from one document instead of resolving the underlying conflict, leaves the actual disagreement between disciplines unresolved.
  12. The correct answer is B (The bioswale's grading, overflow path, and interface with adjacent paving and the building's finished floor elevation): a bioswale functions as a site drainage feature, so its grading, overflow path, and interface with adjacent paving and finished floor elevation are the information that must be coordinated accurately between the architect, landscape architect, and civil engineer. Plant species selection alone does not address whether the feature actually manages stormwater as intended or ties into adjacent grades correctly. Parking lot lighting fixtures and interior lobby finishes are unrelated to the bioswale's function and do not represent the coordination this feature actually requires.
  13. The correct answer is D (Develop a partition type legend keyed on the floor plans, always cross-referenced to detailed partition type drawings for each assembly): documenting several distinct partition assemblies on one floor plan requires a legend keyed to the plan, with each type cross-referenced to a detailed drawing showing its specific assembly, rating, and construction. This lets the contractor and reviewing authority identify exactly which assembly is required at every location on the plan. Describing partitions only in a specification paragraph, using identical wall symbols regardless of rating, or detailing only one partition type all leave the floor plan unable to communicate which assembly belongs where.
  14. The correct answer is C (Confirm the joint accommodates the anticipated structural movement while maintaining weathertight continuity across the material transition): an expansion joint at a bay spacing change and material transition must be sized to accommodate the anticipated structural movement while maintaining a continuous, weathertight seal across the change in exterior material. Confirming only finish color match ignores the structural and envelope performance the joint actually needs to provide. Using a standard catalog width without checking it against the project's actual movement calculations, or assuming the joint can be omitted because the trigger is structural rather than envelope related, both risk a detail that fails to perform as intended.
  15. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: the head, sill, jamb, and anchorage conditions of a punched window in a masonry cavity wall each involve a distinct interface of flashing, structure, and air and water barrier continuity that must be resolved graphically to be buildable and weathertight. Interior finish color and an unrelated break room appliance brand have no bearing on the window's integration with the wall assembly and do not require a detail drawing. Each of the four listed conditions represents a location where water management or structural anchorage could fail if left undetailed. Detailing every true interface of the window assembly, rather than only some of them, is what allows the assembly to perform as designed.
  16. The correct answer is A (Reconcile the door schedule and hardware schedule so both consistently specify the same hardware for that door): when the door schedule and hardware schedule describe different hardware for the same opening, the architect must reconcile the two documents so they consistently specify the same requirement before the set is issued. An unresolved conflict between two schedules the architect prepared invites the contractor to price or install the wrong hardware, and it is the architect's responsibility to catch this before issuance rather than expect the field to guess the intent. Deleting one schedule entirely, or noting the conflict without fixing it, both leave the actual requirement ambiguous.
  17. The correct answer is B (Confirm the selected louver's actual free area and blade configuration provide the airflow the mechanical engineer requires): the architect must confirm that the specific louver selected actually provides the free area and blade configuration the mechanical engineer requires, since a louver's nominal opening size does not equal its actual free area for airflow. Confirming only the exterior color addresses appearance but not whether the equipment will receive adequate airflow. Matching the louver opening to the screen wall's overall dimension, or allowing an unreviewed substitution during construction, both risk a louver that looks correct but fails to meet the required performance.
  18. The correct answer is D (The guardrail height, baluster spacing, and open riser dimensions, confirmed consistent between the stair section and guardrail detail): these dimensions govern life safety compliance and are typically drawn on separate sheets that must still agree with each other, so the architect must confirm them consistent between the stair section and the guardrail detail. Confirming only tread finish, overall stair width, or handrail color addresses appearance rather than the dimensional coordination the two details actually need to share. A monumental stair with a custom guardrail is exactly the condition where a dimensional mismatch between drawings is most likely to occur if not specifically checked.
  19. The correct answer is A (The documents should meet the completeness and clarity a reasonably prudent architect would provide for a comparable project): this is the professional standard of care applied throughout practice, and it scales appropriately to the project's actual complexity rather than requiring an arbitrary maximum or minimum level of detail. Including more detail than a project's complexity calls for wastes effort without improving the set's usefulness. Matching an unrelated project's page count, or minimizing the set to only what a jurisdiction's checklist technically requires, both ignore whether the documents actually communicate the design clearly.
  20. The correct answer is C (Include the drawings needed to communicate the actual scope, such as a demolition plan, partition plan, and door schedule): the architect determines which drawings to include based on what is necessary to communicate the project's actual scope, which for a straightforward partition change means drawings such as a demolition plan, partition plan, and door schedule. Copying a large hospital project's drawing list, or including full structural and mechanical sheets when no such work is occurring, adds unnecessary documents unrelated to actual scope. A single floor plan sheet alone would not adequately communicate demolition extent or hardware requirements for even a simple renovation.
  21. The correct answer is D (Evaluate the cast stone product's performance and dimensions against the original sill detail, and revise details as needed): before accepting a material substitution, the architect must evaluate whether the proposed product actually meets the same performance requirements and dimensions as the original detail, then revise the affected drawings if the substitution is approved. Accepting the change immediately without reviewing the sill detail risks a dimensional or performance mismatch reaching the field. Rejecting the substitution outright without evaluation ignores the possibility that the alternative genuinely performs equivalently, and delaying the decision indefinitely leaves the contractor without direction on which material to install.
  22. The correct answers are A, B, and D: a significant owner-requested scope addition, a jurisdiction-mandated redesign of the means of egress after plan review, and a mid-project change in delivery method each represent substantive changes beyond the original scope and effort the architect priced into basic services. A routine contractor clarification about information already clearly shown, a consultant confirming a meeting time, and correcting a minor typo during internal review are all ordinary parts of basic services and do not represent added scope. The distinguishing factor is whether the event requires meaningful additional design or documentation effort rather than simply routine communication or self-correction. Recognizing which category a given event falls into is what allows the architect to notify the owner appropriately rather than either over-claiming or absorbing real added scope.
  23. The correct answer is B (Document the scope change in writing, revise the affected drawings, and evaluate the impact on schedule and fee): a significant owner-added scope item such as a new rooftop penthouse should be documented in writing, incorporated into the affected drawings, and evaluated for its impact on schedule and fee before proceeding. A written record protects both the architect and owner by establishing what was requested and when. Absorbing the change into the drawings with no written record, waiting until construction begins to add it, or refusing to document it until a separate signed amendment arrives all fail to manage the change through a clear, contemporaneous process.
  24. The correct answer is C (Review each comment, confirm the intended change with the owner if unclear, and revise the affected drawings accordingly): the architect should review each owner comment individually, confirm the intended change directly with the owner where the markup is unclear, and revise the affected drawings as part of normal document development. Ignoring comments based on the architect's own unilateral judgment of significance risks missing something the owner considers important. Waiting until the 100% set to address 50% comments delays needed clarification, and forwarding markup directly to the contractor bypasses the architect's role in interpreting and incorporating the owner's design intent.
  25. The correct answer is A (Communicate the resulting schedule impact to the owner and coordinate a revised issuance date for the full set): when a late change causes a real delay to another discipline's drawings, the architect must communicate the resulting schedule impact to the owner and coordinate a revised issuance date for the coordinated set. Issuing the architectural drawings on the original schedule while the structural drawings remain incomplete would release an uncoordinated set. Assuming a delay affecting one discipline has no effect on the overall schedule ignores that the full set typically issues together, and instructing the engineer to skip needed revisions would leave the design incomplete rather than resolve the delay.
  26. The correct answer is D (Yes, because preparing an entirely new drawing set to comply with a code adopted after the project began exceeds scope): preparing an entirely new drawing set to comply with a code amendment adopted after the project began, and after the original documents were already issued for permit, represents work beyond what the architect's original scope and fee anticipated. Responding to typical plan review comments is part of basic services, but a full new set triggered by a change in applicable law after project commencement is a different scale of effort. Accessibility compliance remains an architectural responsibility even after documents are issued, and the architect's personal agreement or disagreement with the requirement has no bearing on whether the added work exceeds scope.
  27. The correct answer is B (Yes, provided the architect's drawings clearly establish design intent and performance requirements the shop drawings must satisfy): referencing a roofing subcontractor's shop drawings for final attachment patterns is appropriate as long as the architect's own drawings still establish the design intent and performance requirements those shop drawings must satisfy. This preserves the architect's responsibility for overall design while allowing installation-level detail to be delegated, consistent with other delegated design arrangements common on design-build projects. Requiring the architect to independently detail every fastener pattern duplicates work appropriately delegated elsewhere, while removing all roofing information from the documents would leave no performance basis for reviewing the shop drawings against.
  28. The correct answer is A (Revise the reflected ceiling plan and any related sections to show the coordinated, updated corridor ceiling height): once the mechanical engineer's duct layout requires a taller corridor ceiling, the reflected ceiling plan and any related sections must be revised to show the coordinated, updated height so the drawings reflect the actual condition being built. Leaving the earlier design development height unchanged because it was previously reviewed ignores that the design has since changed for a documented coordination reason. Showing two conflicting heights on separate sheets, or removing the dimension, both leave the contractor without one clear height to build to.
  29. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: updating the finish schedule with final material, manufacturer, and color, coordinating that schedule with the specifications, showing finish transitions on the relevant plans, and confirming special installation requirements at finish boundaries are all necessary to communicate a finalized finish selection accurately across the document set. Leaving the schedule blank and relying on a verbal description given during one site visit provides no reliable, coordinated record for bidders or the contractor. Changing an unrelated structural drawing to reference a carpet color has no bearing on the interior finish and does not belong on that document. Together, these actions ensure the finish selection is represented consistently everywhere a bidder or installer would look for it.
  30. The correct answer is D (Verify the discharge point, flow path, and quantity are consistent between the roof plan and the civil drainage plan): the architect must verify that the discharge point, flow path, and quantity of water leaving the roof scupper are consistent between the roof plan and the civil engineer's drainage plan, since the water crosses from architectural to civil documentation at that point. Verifying only the scupper's finish color, or its location on the roof plan alone but not where the water goes, leaves the coordination between disciplines incomplete. Matching a generic industry standard drain size instead of the project's actual calculated roof area risks a system that does not match the design conditions.
  31. The correct answer is C (Coordinate with the electrical engineer and civil engineer to resolve the conflict and confirm a location that works): resolving a conflict between the electrical engineer's transformer location and the architect's accessible parking space requires coordinating with both the electrical and civil engineers to find a location that satisfies all requirements. Moving the transformer directly on the architectural sheet without discussing it with the electrical engineer risks a location that does not meet clearance or utility requirements. Simply deleting the accessible parking space, or leaving both conflicting locations shown for the contractor to sort out, both avoid resolving the actual design conflict.
  32. The correct answer is B (A dimensioned plan and profile showing the route's slope, cross slope, and any required landings along its length): demonstrating an accessible route across a change in grade requires a dimensioned plan and profile showing the route's running slope, cross slope, and any landings required along its length, since these are the specific dimensions accessibility requirements govern. A general note stating a route exists somewhere provides no verifiable dimensional information. A single photograph or a verbal description given during a site walk likewise fail to establish the measurable slope and landing information a reviewing authority or contractor needs to confirm compliance.
  33. The correct answer is D (Confirm the guardrail, structural design, and site grading are coordinated and consistently documented across the drawings): a retaining wall exceeding four feet along an accessible route typically triggers coordinated guardrail, structural, and grading requirements that must all be consistently documented across the civil and architectural drawings. Confirming only the wall's finish material ignores the life safety and structural implications of its height. Arbitrarily reducing the shown height below the trigger threshold regardless of actual site grading misrepresents the real condition, and omitting the wall from the architectural drawings entirely fails to coordinate the guardrail requirement it creates.
  34. The correct answer is A (Confirm the roof plan, structural, and electrical drawings consistently show the panel layout avoiding the reserved equipment area): a rooftop solar array constrained by both structural capacity and a reserved future equipment area requires the roof plan, structural, and electrical drawings to consistently show the panel layout avoiding that reserved area. Showing the layout only on the electrical drawings ignores that the structural engineer's capacity limit is what constrains where panels can actually go. Ignoring the reserved area because future equipment is not yet designed risks a conflict once that equipment is eventually installed, and omitting the array location leaves the coordination undocumented.
  35. The correct answer is B (The tested assembly reference, layer thickness, and fire-stopping at penetrations that always establish the required one-hour rating): a fire-rated partition type detail must convey the tested assembly reference, layer thickness, and fire-stopping requirements at penetrations, since these are the specific elements that establish and maintain the required rating. Finish selection, furniture layout, and lighting fixture type are all appearance or programming decisions unrelated to whether the partition actually achieves and maintains its fire rating. Without the tested assembly information, a contractor cannot confirm the wall as built will satisfy the rating the design requires.
Case Study PDD_PM_C1

An architect of record is finalizing the project manual for a six-story mixed-use building with ground-floor retail and five levels of residential units above. The project is being delivered through a competitive bid process using a stipulated-sum contract. During a coordination review before bid issuance, the specification writer flags that the curtain wall specification section calls for a one-hour fire-resistance rating at the floor line, while the enlarged wall section on the construction drawings shows a spandrel panel assembly detailed for a two-hour rating consistent with the building's occupancy separation requirements. The contractor's bid package will include the project manual, covering Divisions 00 through 01 plus the technical specifications, the drawings, and any addenda issued before bid opening. The owner has also asked that all curtain wall assemblies be supported by independent laboratory fire test reports rather than manufacturer literature alone, and that any substitution requests be handled through a formal written procedure rather than informal phone calls to the architect. The architect must resolve the discrepancy between the drawings and specifications, confirm which CSI division governs the curtain wall system, verify the testing protocol required to substantiate the fire rating, and determine where in the project manual the substitution procedure should be documented so bidders understand the requirement before submitting their proposals.

  1. The correct answer is A (Issue an addendum reconciling the specification and drawings to one consistent fire-resistance rating before bidding): a competitive stipulated-sum bid requires every bidder to price identical scope, so any known discrepancy between the drawings and specifications must be resolved through a formal addendum before bid opening. Deleting the rating from the specification would strip out the reference standard and testing language bidders need to price the assembly correctly. Letting the low bidder pick the cheaper rating would place a life-safety, code-driven decision in the hands of the party with the least incentive to choose the safer option. Waiting for a request for information during construction invites inconsistent bids, change orders, and schedule delay that proper pre-bid coordination avoids.
  2. The correct answer is C (Division 08, Openings, section 08 44 00, the section written specifically for curtain wall and glazed assemblies): MasterFormat places glazed curtain wall and storefront systems in Division 08 because the assembly's primary function is an operable or fixed opening in the building envelope, even though it incorporates metal framing and provides weather protection. Division 05 covers structural and miscellaneous metals, not glazed envelope systems. Division 07 covers thermal and moisture protection products like roofing and waterproofing membranes, not glazed wall assemblies. Division 09 covers interior and exterior finishes applied to substrates, not the structural glazed system itself.
  3. The correct answer is B (ASTM E119, the standard fire test of building construction and materials for rated assemblies): ASTM E119 is the recognized test method for establishing the fire-resistance rating, in hours, of building assemblies such as the spandrel panel at the floor line, and an independent laboratory report against this standard is what substantiates the two-hour requirement. ASTM E84 measures surface burning characteristics like flame spread of interior finish materials, not the fire-resistance rating of an assembly. NFPA 285 addresses fire propagation through combustible components within an exterior wall, a related but separate test from the assembly's hourly rating. ASTM E90 measures acoustic sound transmission loss and has nothing to do with fire resistance.
  4. The correct answer is B (Division 01, General Requirements, in a dedicated substitution procedures section such as 01 25 00): the standard MasterFormat convention places the formal, project-wide substitution request procedure in Division 01 so it governs consistently across every technical section rather than being buried or repeated inconsistently. Placing it only within the instructions to bidders exhibit limits it to the bidding phase and leaves no procedure for substitutions requested during construction. The general conditions of the contract address broader legal relationships, not the specific documentation steps for a substitution request. Repeating the procedure inside every technical section invites inconsistency and contradicts the purpose of a single, centrally located Division 01 requirement.
  5. The correct answer is D (In Division 00, Procurement Requirements, within the bid form or bidding requirements section): bonding requirements and bond amounts are procurement information that bidders must know before pricing their proposal, so the standard convention places this in Division 00 rather than in the technical specifications or general conditions. A cover sheet note on the drawings is not the recognized location for procurement or contractual bonding requirements and is easy for a bidder to overlook. Burying the requirement within the supplementary conditions separates it from the bid form bidders are actually completing. Division 01 addresses general administrative requirements for construction, not the bidding-phase bonding requirement.
  6. The correct answer is A (Manufacturer names, material composition, panel gauge, finish coatings, and fastener types for the roofing system): Part 2, Products, of the standard three-part specification format describes the actual materials, components, and manufactured products required, including gauge, finish, and fastener types. Submittal and quality assurance requirements, including mock-ups and reference standards, belong in Part 1, General. Surface preparation, installation sequence, and field quality control describe the work performed on site and belong in Part 3, Execution. Related sections, administrative requirements, and definitions are also Part 1, General content that establishes the section's scope before products or execution are addressed.
  7. The correct answer is C (A performance specification stating the required noise reduction coefficient and letting the contractor select a compliant product): a performance specification states the measurable outcome required, such as a target noise reduction coefficient, while giving the contractor and manufacturer flexibility in how that outcome is achieved. A proprietary specification names a single approved product and removes the flexibility the architect wants to allow. A descriptive specification locks in exact composition and manufacturing details, which is the opposite of giving the manufacturer flexibility. A reference standard specification without any numeric target would not establish the specific noise reduction coefficient the project requires.
  8. The correct answer is B (Revise the hardware specification to include the panic hardware set so the drawings and specification agree): panic hardware at an egress stair door is typically a code-driven life-safety requirement, so the specification must be corrected to match the drawings rather than allowing the discrepancy to reach bidders or the field. Relying on a precedence clause does not actually fix the missing scope and risks a contractor pricing without the required hardware. Leaving the conflict for the hardware supplier to interpret shifts a design decision to a party not responsible for code compliance. Deleting the panic hardware note from the drawings would remove a required life-safety feature rather than correcting the actual error.
  9. The correct answers are A, B, C, and D: a complete project manual for a competitively bid project organizes bidding requirements, contract forms, general conditions, and supplementary conditions ahead of the technical specifications so procurement, contractual, and administrative requirements are clearly established before the trade-specific sections begin. Structural calculations prepared for permit submission are engineering work product used to verify code compliance, not a standard project manual component distributed to bidders. A raw geotechnical borings log in unedited laboratory format is not how subsurface information is typically incorporated, since the report is usually referenced or attached as a defined exhibit rather than included as unprocessed data.
  10.
