The correct answer is B (Determine system compatible with loads): Once the existing structure's capacity and condition are assessed, the next step is determining a new structural system that is compatible with the loads and connections the existing structure can accept, since this directly depends on what the assessment reveals. Coordinating connections between old and new construction cannot happen until a compatible new system has been selected. Obtaining structural engineer sign-off is the last step in the sequence, after connections are coordinated. Assessing the existing structure's condition is the first step, which this question already places before the step in question.
  137. The correct answer is A (It requires the building's core to grow, reducing the leasable floor plate around it.): adding elevators to meet peak traffic demand requires additional shaft space within the building's core, and because the core typically cannot expand without reducing the surrounding leasable area, the tower's floor plate configuration is directly affected. This finding does not eliminate the need for a freight elevator, reduce floor-to-floor height, or require a change in the primary structural material.
  138. The correct answer is B (Relocating the mechanical room or adding a buffer zone and isolation between the two spaces): the most effective way to resolve a direct adjacency between noisy mechanical equipment and a space requiring very low background noise is to relocate the equipment or introduce a buffer zone and acoustic isolation between the two, since noise transmission depends heavily on distance, mass, and separation. Relocating the recital hall's entrance, increasing its occupant load, or simply reducing equipment capacity without changing its location would not adequately resolve the acoustic conflict.
  139. The correct answer is D (That the roof structure can carry the added dead load of the panels and mounting racks.): a large photovoltaic array adds significant additional dead load from the panels and their mounting system, and since the original roof was only sized for a standard minimum live load, the team must verify the structure can safely carry this added weight before installation. Loading dock door width, the site's parking ratio, and the type of fire suppression agent used are unrelated to whether the roof can structurally support the array.
  140. The correct answer is A (By stepping the building's floor levels to follow the site's natural grade change.): an eighteen-foot grade change across a building footprint is significant enough that stepping the floor levels to follow the natural slope is a common and efficient configuration response, reducing excavation and allowing the building to work with the topography rather than against it. Forcing the entire building onto one flat level, relocating the project elsewhere, or eliminating below-grade space entirely are far less practical responses to this degree of site slope.
  141. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: the required span lengths and expected gravity and lateral loads set the basic structural demands the system must satisfy. The local availability and cost of qualified labor and materials affects what is practical and economical to build in this region, and the applicable seismic design category along with site-specific soil conditions shapes which systems will perform adequately. The chosen system also directly affects the building's overall configuration and floor-to-floor height, so that impact belongs in the evaluation as well. A corporate logo color and a tenant lounge coffee brand have no bearing on which structural system is appropriate for the building.
  142. The correct answer is B (How the change affects floor-to-floor height, fire protection, and previously coordinated MEP routing.): switching primary structural materials this late in design can change member depths, fire protection requirements, and construction sequencing, all of which can disrupt MEP routing and floor-to-floor heights that were already coordinated with the original system. The building's street address, landscaping plan, and signage package approval are administrative matters unrelated to this kind of structural system change.
  143. The correct answer is C (It requires dedicated shaft space within or near the core for the pressurization ductwork.): a stair pressurization system needs a fan and a duct network to actively supply air into the stair enclosure, and that ductwork requires dedicated shaft space typically located within or adjacent to the structural core. This requirement does not remove the need for a rated stair enclosure, increase floor-to-floor height uniformly across the building, or force every elevator outside the core.
  144. The correct answer is D (By reserving a dedicated, accessible shaft that stacks consistently through every patient floor.): medical gas piping is a life-safety system that must remain accessible for periodic testing and maintenance, so the building's vertical chase configuration needs a dedicated shaft that stacks consistently through every patient floor served. This requirement does not eliminate the need for other mechanical shafts, allow the piping to run exposed in corridors contrary to code, or reduce how many patient rooms a floor can serve.
  145. The correct answer is B (Flexible connections or fittings at each system that crosses the expansion joint.): because the structural expansion joint allows the two wings to move independently in response to thermal expansion and contraction, every duct, pipe, and conduit crossing that joint needs a flexible connection or fitting designed to accommodate that same relative movement without failing. A rigid, fixed connection would be damaged by the joint's movement, and an extra fire-rated shaft or a size reduction at the crossing does not address the movement issue at all.
  146. The correct answer is A (The floor above may have reduced ceiling height where the transfer beam occurs.): picking up a column with a transfer beam one level above means that beam now occupies structural depth within the ceiling plenum of the floor directly above, which can reduce the finished ceiling height in that area unless the design compensates for it. This change does not add usable floor area, lower the required occupant load, or remove the need for sprinkler coverage on that floor.
  147. The correct answer is C (The relative local availability and cost of each material in this region.): beyond meeting structural requirements, material selection is strongly influenced by what is locally available and economical, and in a region where timber is harvested locally while steel must be shipped a long distance, that availability and cost difference should heavily influence the framing selection. Exterior panel color, parking space counts, and interior signage style are unrelated to the structural material decision.
  148. The correct answer is A (Conduit routing may conflict with already-placed structural members and ceiling systems above corridors): adding a comprehensive security system's conduit network after the structural and ceiling systems are already finalized means the new conduit runs must be threaded through existing conditions, creating a real risk of conflicts with structural members and ceiling systems that were coordinated without this later addition in mind. This late addition does not require recalculating occupant load from scratch, increasing the parking ratio, or redesigning the fire suppression water supply.
  149. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: wider column spacing changes where columns land, so the architect must confirm the new grid still supports the planned tenant layouts and the location of the building's core. Wider spans generally require deeper beams, which can reduce ceiling height and constrain duct routing, so that tradeoff needs evaluation as well. A change of this scale can also affect the overall project cost and schedule, and it must still satisfy the applicable structural code requirements before being accepted. An exterior signage font and a new street tree species are unrelated finish and landscape decisions that do not depend on the structural column spacing.
  150. The correct answer is D (It requires the parapet height and scupper elevations to be set to control overflow): overflow scuppers function as a secondary drainage path only if their elevation and the surrounding parapet height are coordinated to allow water to escape before it rises to a level that would overload the roof structure, so both dimensions must be deliberately set as part of the roof configuration. This requirement does not eliminate the parapet, relocate the primary drains into the parapet, or reduce the mechanical penthouse's required height.
  151. The correct answer is C (Configuration should resolve program, code, systems, and site conditions together, since each constrains the others): building configuration is not determined by any single factor in isolation. The program's spatial needs, the code's height and area limits, the selected structural and mechanical systems, and the site's environmental conditions all constrain one another, so a workable configuration has to reconcile them together rather than resolving one and adjusting the rest afterward. Letting each consultant work independently, following the program alone, or defaulting only to code minimums would each risk producing a configuration that fails to satisfy the other constraints.
Case Study PPD_BC_C2

A design team is developing a four-story outpatient surgical pavilion above two levels of below-grade parking on a tight urban infill site. The parking levels are most efficient on a 60-foot by 60-foot structural bay split by a center row of columns into two 27-foot clear-span double-loaded aisles, but the second-floor operating suite requires column-free spans of at least 40 feet to keep sterile corridors and OR footprints free of intruding structure. The architect is also resolving vertical stacking: the mechanical engineer wants medical gas and exhaust risers grouped in a single shaft aligned with the OR suite above, but the developer has asked that upper floors retain flexibility for future conversion to general medical office space if surgical volume declines. Floor-to-floor height is constrained to 14 feet by zoning height limits, which leaves limited room for structural transfer members without compressing ceiling plenum space needed for OR-specific air handling. The structural engineer has proposed a transfer girder at the second floor to reconcile the parking grid below with the wider OR spans above, while the project's cost consultant has flagged that transfer structure adds significant cost and schedule risk. The team must also decide where to locate the elevator and stair core relative to the shaft, since relocating the core later to accommodate a future tenant fit-out would be far more disruptive than relocating a duct run.

  1. The correct answer is A (Introduce a transfer structure at the second floor to widen the bay spacing above): a transfer girder lets the parking level below keep its efficient 60-foot by 60-foot column grid while the OR suite above gets the wider 40-foot clear span it needs, even though the parking level's own clear span between columns is only 27 feet. This preserves parking efficiency without compromising the sterile field. Reducing the OR span or eliminating parking sacrifices program function, and raising the floor-to-floor height throughout the building adds cost everywhere rather than solving the local conflict.
  2. The correct answer is C (Locate the shaft near the core, sized slightly larger for future systems): keeping the shaft adjacent to the vertical circulation core supports short, efficient duct runs to the OR suite while a modest size increase leaves room for future office-conversion systems without relocating the shaft later. Sizing the shaft only for current OR needs forecloses future flexibility, scattering risers across the floor defeats centralized distribution, and routing gas lines through the open plenum instead of a shaft is not a viable strategy for pressurized medical gas systems.
  3. The correct answer is B (A duct run can shift within the plenum, but the core's geometry is fixed once built): ductwork lives within a flexible ceiling cavity and can be rerouted during a later renovation, while the structural and egress geometry of an elevator and stair core is effectively permanent once the building is constructed. This makes an early, well-considered core location far more consequential than a single duct routing decision. The other options either overstate a minor mechanical inconvenience or mischaracterize the structural and code significance of vertical circulation cores.
  4. The correct answer is D (The ceiling plenum available for OR air handling would shrink accordingly): with floor-to-floor height fixed at 14 feet, any increase in transfer girder depth directly reduces the plenum space remaining for the OR suite's air handling ductwork above it. This is a direct example of how a structural cost-saving decision in one system can compromise a mechanical requirement in an adjacent system. The clear span requirement, the parking level's own height, and the shaft's bay location are not the systems most immediately affected by a deeper girder at that specific floor.
  5. The correct answer is B (Place the lobby and lecture hall in a separate bay outside the lab module): isolating the open, column-free program into its own structural bay lets the lab module keep its tightly spaced, vibration-controlled grid without either program compromising the other. This avoids the cost and complexity of a full transfer structure. Forcing shared grids in either direction, whether tightening the lobby or loosening the lab, degrades one program's core functional requirement.
  6. The correct answer is D (Locate shafts along a shared core wall so floor plates can subdivide freely): placing mechanical shafts along a fixed core wall keeps distribution centralized for efficient duct runs while leaving the surrounding floor plate open for tenants to subdivide in whatever configuration future leasing requires. A single center shaft still constrains subdivision around it, shafts embedded in a specific tenant suite lock in that suite's boundaries, and horizontal-only distribution from the perimeter is inefficient and impractical at building scale.
  7. The correct answers are A, C, and D: a building's overall configuration should be resolved from the structural system's real bay spacing and capacity, from code-driven requirements like occupant load and egress width, and from the client's actual program adjacencies and functional relationships, since these are the technical and functional drivers NCARB identifies for this task. A contractor's bidding schedule, interior finish colors, and a marketing timeline may matter to the project overall, but none of them should drive the building's structural, code, or programmatic configuration.
  8. The correct answer is A (Use a slab-mounted fan coil system with a shallower profile per floor): a shallower mechanical system reduces the plenum depth required at every floor, freeing up floor-to-floor height for additional guest room levels within the fixed zoning limit. Exceeding the zoning height limit is not a permitted option, eliminating ducted systems removes a functional requirement rather than resolving it, and reducing the guest room count sacrifices program to avoid solving the actual configuration conflict.
  9. The correct answer is C (Locate the two stairs at opposite ends of the core, leaving space open): placing the two required exit stairs at opposite ends of a shared core satisfies the code requirement for remote egress locations while keeping the floor plate between them fully open for tenant flexibility. Clustering stairs together fails the remoteness requirement, eliminating a required stair violates code outright, and scattering many stairs across the plate defeats the goal of an open, flexible floor.
  10. The correct answer is B (Narrow the floor plate or add a central light well for daylight): reducing the effective floor plate depth, either by narrowing the footprint or introducing a light well, brings daylight further into the plan and reduces the cooling load the sustainability consultant flagged. Relying entirely on artificial systems ignores the recommendation, confining daylight programs to the corners underuses the rest of the floor, and glazing only one facade does not address the deep floor plate itself.
  11. The correct answer is D (Align the new grid's column spacing with the historic facade's bay rhythm): matching the new structural grid to the protected facade's existing bay rhythm satisfies the preservation ordinance while still giving the structural engineer a coordinated, buildable grid at the shared elevation. Ignoring the rhythm, demolishing protected fabric, or artificially separating the cornice lines all fail to reconcile the new structure with the preservation requirement.
  12. The correct answer is B (Whether the applicable code or manufacturer requires retention for this height and exposure): whether a secondary mechanical retention system is required for structural silicone glazing depends on the applicable building code provisions and the manufacturer's requirements for the specific glazing height and exposure conditions of the project. Claiming retention systems are not required is incorrect, since many codes and manufacturers do require them above certain heights or wind exposure thresholds. The glazing color has no bearing on whether a structural safety retention system is required. A manufacturer's warranty terms are a separate commercial consideration from the code-driven retention requirement the architect must verify.
  13. The correct answer is C (Have the structural engineer verify the slab for the pedestal point loads): because the existing slab was not designed for the concentrated point loads that raised access floor pedestals introduce, the structural engineer must verify the slab can carry those loads before the system is finalized. Proceeding on the assumption that pedestals distribute load broadly is an unconfirmed assumption rather than an engineering verification. Eliminating the raised floor and routing cabling overhead abandons the design approach rather than confirming whether the original approach can be made to work. Spacing the pedestals further apart changes the load pattern but does not substitute for the structural engineer's verification of the slab's actual capacity.
  14. The correct answer is D (The 3.5-ton unit, matching the calculated load exactly): dividing the calculated cooling load of 42,000 Btu per hour by 12,000 Btu per hour per ton yields exactly 3.5 tons, matching the calculated load precisely among the available equipment sizes. A smaller unit rounds the calculated load down and would be undersized relative to the actual demand. Efficiency ratings are a separate consideration from whether a unit's capacity matches the calculated load, so selecting an undersized unit for that reason still leaves it insufficient. A larger unit adds more capacity than the calculated load requires, which is not the closest match among the standard sizes available.
  15. The correct answer is A (Coordinate a dedicated ventilation or cooling system sized for the equipment's heat output): elevator machine room equipment generates significant heat that a single unconditioned exterior louver cannot reliably dissipate, so the architect must coordinate a dedicated ventilation or cooling system sized to the equipment's actual heat output before construction documents are issued. Relying on the exterior louver alone ignores the heat load stated for this specific equipment. Relocating the machine room to the roof does not address the underlying ventilation and cooling need without further mechanical review. Increasing only the louver size without evaluating the actual heat output does not confirm the room will be adequately cooled.
  16. The correct answer is B (Whether a structural connector is needed for the panels to act together): because the structural engineer's design assumes the precast panels act together to resist lateral load, the architect must confirm whether a structural connector, not just a sealant joint, is needed at the vertical joint for the panels to actually behave as the structural design assumes. Claiming sealant joints are adequate for all precast connections ignores the specific structural assumption stated for this joint. Sealant color coordination is a finish consideration unrelated to whether the joint can transfer the lateral load the structural design requires. Widening the joint for easier sealant application does not address whether a structural connection is needed at all.
  17. The correct answer is C (The 2-inch pipe, since it comfortably exceeds the calculated flow at the velocity limit): the 2-inch pipe's capacity of approximately 78 gpm at the 8 feet per second maximum velocity criterion exceeds the branch's calculated 60 gpm flow requirement, satisfying the velocity limit at the required flow. A smaller pipe's capacity of only about 44 gpm at that same velocity limit falls short of the 60 gpm required, making it undersized. Increasing the flow rate through the smaller pipe beyond its velocity limit would create excessive pressure drop and noise. Downsizing the pipe in the field based on unconfirmed measured flow abandons the calculated design flow that has already been established.
  18. The correct answer is A (A listed smoke damper installed at the rated wall penetration, per code): a duct penetrating a wall that functions as both a fire-rated corridor wall and a smoke barrier requires a listed smoke damper at the penetration, since sheet metal ductwork alone does not restore the wall's required smoke and fire separation. A heavier gauge duct without a separate smoke damper does not provide the tested, listed protection code requires at this type of penetration. A fire-rated duct wrap along the duct's horizontal run addresses a different condition and does not substitute for the smoke damper required specifically at the rated wall penetration. Claiming no additional protection is needed ignores that the wall was identified as a code-required smoke barrier.
  19. The correct answers are A, B, and C: before approving new rooftop antenna equipment, the architect should coordinate a structural evaluation of the roof for the added dead and wind loads, a cabling pathway that does not compromise existing roof penetrations, and verification that the equipment does not obstruct required roof access or egress paths. Matching the equipment's color to the roofing membrane is an aesthetic preference with no bearing on the structural or life-safety coordination required. Eliminating the roof warranty inspection requirement entirely removes a protection the owner relies on rather than addressing the installation's actual impacts. Maximizing citywide signal strength is the telecommunications provider's technical concern, not a coordination item the architect is responsible for.
  20. The correct answer is D (That the drift zone framing and roofing details reflect the actual local drift load): because the calculated local snow drift load of 45 pounds per square foot significantly exceeds the uniform 20 pound per square foot load used elsewhere, the architect must confirm the drift zone's framing and roofing details actually reflect this higher, localized load value. Suggesting the roofing membrane color reduces snow accumulation does not address the structural load the drift condition creates. Ignoring the drift condition as a localized effect disregards a real, calculated load that must be designed for at that specific zone. Uniformly upgrading the entire roof structure to the drift load value is unnecessary and costly when only the localized drift zone requires the higher design load.
  21. The correct answer is C (Water reaching the sill has no dedicated drainage path away from the opening): without a dedicated flashing membrane at the sill, water reaching this location through incidental moisture or minor cladding imperfections has no dedicated path to drain away from the rough opening, risking water intrusion into the wall assembly. Finish coat color mismatch is a cosmetic issue unrelated to the actual moisture management concern at this detail. Whether the operable sash clears the assembly's thickness at the jamb is a separate clearance issue from the sill drainage path in question. Visible fasteners through the finish coat is also a cosmetic concern, not the primary functional problem with omitting sill flashing.
  22. The correct answer is B (The conductor's ampacity is below the breaker rating and must be resolved): the copper conductor's 35-amp ampacity rating is below the 40-amp breaker protecting the circuit, which is a code violation since the overcurrent protection must not exceed the conductor's ampacity, and the architect must flag this mismatch for correction. Dismissing ampacity ratings as approximate and rarely enforced ignores that conductor sizing is a code-mandated safety requirement. Simply documenting the breaker size in the architectural drawings does not resolve the underlying mismatch between the conductor and its overcurrent protection. Conductor insulation color coordination with panel labeling is an installation convenience, not a substitute for correcting an undersized conductor.
  23. The correct answer is D (That the detail clearly dimensions the embedment depth the calculations assumed): because the structural engineer's calculations depended on a specific anchor bolt embedment depth, the architect must confirm the detail clearly dimensions that embedment depth so the connection is built to match the assumptions the calculations were based on. Matching the baseplate's steel grade to the column's grade is a material specification detail separate from the embedment depth question. Painting the anchor bolts for corrosion protection is a finish consideration unrelated to whether the embedment depth is properly dimensioned. Matching the baseplate size to the column footprint does not confirm the embedment depth the structural calculations require.
  24. The correct answer is A (Confirm the generator is resized to serve the full 105 kW combined load): because code requires the smoke control fan to run on emergency power along with the life-safety loads, the total demand becomes 105 kW, and the architect must confirm the generator is resized to serve this full combined load rather than the original 85 kW alone. Keeping the generator at 85 kW ignores the code requirement that the smoke control fan also operate on emergency power. Adding a second, separate generator is an unnecessarily costly approach when resizing the single generator can serve both loads. Reducing the life-safety load calculation to make room for the fan's demand would improperly understate a code-required load rather than accommodating the actual combined demand.
  25. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a detail resolving a corner where roof, curtain wall, and structural steel framing converge must address continuity of the roof membrane and curtain wall air barrier, the structural connection transferring curtain wall loads into the framing, and thermal bridging where the structural steel penetrates the insulated envelope. Finish color coordination between the roofing and curtain wall systems is an aesthetic matter, not a performance requirement this detail must resolve. Warranty duration for each product is a commercial term unrelated to how the systems physically integrate at this detail. Construction schedule sequencing for unrelated interior finish trades has no bearing on how this exterior envelope detail itself must be resolved.
  26. The correct answer is B (That the duct placement leaves the required concrete cover above it intact): because maintaining the composite metal deck's structural action depends on the concrete cover specified above the underfloor ducts, the architect must verify the duct placement within the 4.5-inch slab leaves the required 3-inch cover intact. Eliminating the underfloor duct system in favor of surface raceways abandons the intended distribution strategy rather than confirming whether it can work within the slab as designed. Reducing the total slab depth to lower the building's height would reduce the concrete cover further, worsening rather than resolving the coordination issue. Relocating the duct system entirely below the structural steel deck changes the system's location rather than confirming the cover requirement within the specified slab.
  27. The correct answer is C (Relocate the sensor along the ceiling to a position with a clear view): relocating the photosensor along the ceiling to a position with a clear, unobstructed view of the skylight's daylight pattern allows the daylighting control system to function as intended without conflicting with the structural beam's fixed location. Removing the skylight eliminates the daylighting strategy entirely rather than resolving a solvable coordination conflict. Mounting the sensor on the beam's underside while ignoring the obstructed sightline would leave the sensor unable to accurately read the daylight condition it is meant to control. Disabling the daylighting control feature for the nearest fixtures abandons the energy strategy instead of relocating the sensor to a workable position.
Case Study PDD_IBM_C2

A design team is developing construction documents for a five-story mixed-use building with ground-floor retail, four floors of open-plan office space above, a mechanical penthouse, and an occupied vegetated roof terrace. The building uses a unitized curtain wall system anchored to structural steel spandrel beams at each floor line, and the structural engineer has specified a steel moment frame with beam depths that leave only 14 inches of usable space above each finished ceiling for mechanical, electrical, and plumbing distribution. The mechanical engineer has selected a variable refrigerant flow system with refrigerant piping and condensate lines that must run continuously from the penthouse to terminal units on every floor. During coordination review, the architect discovers that a required fire protection standpipe, sized by the fire protection engineer, is shown running directly through the same shaft the elevator consultant has allocated for a traction elevator's overhead sheave assembly. Separately, the landscape architect's vegetated roof terrace design specifies a 10-inch growing medium depth with saturated soil weight that was not included in the structural engineer's original roof live load assumptions. The architect must resolve each conflict while preserving the design intent of the curtain wall, ceiling heights, and roof terrace program.

  1. The correct answer is B (Design curtain wall anchors with slotted holes that allow vertical slip while transferring lateral wind loads to the spandrel beams.): slotted anchor holes let the curtain wall slip vertically relative to the steel frame as the structure deflects and moves thermally, while the anchor still transfers lateral wind load into the spandrel beam. Welding the anchor plates directly to the beam flange eliminates any capacity for movement, forcing structural deflection directly into the glazing system and risking cracked glass or bent frames. A rigid grout bed similarly locks the two systems together with no give. Simply increasing the spandrel beam depth reduces deflection but does not eliminate the need to detail for the differential movement that will still occur.
  2. The correct answer is A (Request that the structural engineer identify approved beam web penetration locations near the neutral axis and away from high-shear regions near the supports.): penetrating a beam web near its neutral axis, where bending stress is lowest, and away from high-shear regions near the supports, preserves the member's structural capacity while allowing the mechanical piping to pass through the framing depth. Field cutting penetrations without engineering review risks compromising the beam's shear and moment capacity at an uncontrolled location. Reducing the ceiling height further worsens the already tight plenum condition without resolving the routing conflict. Eliminating the VRF system for ductwork does not solve the coordination problem, since ductwork typically requires even more depth than refrigerant piping.
  3. The correct answer is D (Convene the fire protection engineer and elevator consultant to relocate the standpipe to an adjacent chase clear of the sheave assembly.): a shared shaft containing both a code required standpipe and fixed elevator equipment needs the responsible consultants at the table to relocate one system to a clear chase, since neither element can simply be resized or removed to fit. Asking the elevator consultant to shrink the sheave assembly is not realistic, since that equipment is manufactured to fixed dimensions for the traction system selected. Removing the standpipe and relying on portable extinguishers abandons a code required fire protection system. Shrinking the shaft width only on the architectural plans does not resolve the actual physical conflict between the two systems.
  4. The correct answer is C (Provide the structural engineer with the saturated soil unit weight and growing medium depth so the roof deck and framing can be reanalyzed.): since the saturated growing medium weight was never part of the original roof live load assumptions, the structural engineer needs the actual unit weight and depth data to reanalyze the deck and framing for the real load condition. Reducing the growing medium depth to match the old assumptions avoids doing the recalculation rather than confirming the roof can actually carry the design. Adding a disclaimer note and leaving verification to the contractor does not fulfill the architect's coordination responsibility. Substituting synthetic turf without informing the landscape architect unilaterally changes an approved design without proper consultant coordination.
  5. The correct answer is C (Introduce a continuous polyamide thermal break within the mullion profile to interrupt the aluminum's thermal conductivity path.): a continuous thermal break interrupts the highly conductive aluminum frame, raising the interior mullion surface temperature above the dew point and directly addressing the conduction path causing condensation. Raising interior humidity would lower the dew point margin and actually increase, not reduce, condensation risk. A darker anodized finish changes solar absorption, which has no bearing on the conductive heat loss occurring at the interior mullion surface. Reducing the glass unit size does not change the thermal conductivity of the aluminum framing itself, which remains the source of the condensation.
  6. The correct answer is B (Lap the roofing membrane up and over the parapet, tying it into the curtain wall head flashing before the coping is installed.): lapping the membrane continuously into the head flashing before the coping goes on maintains one unbroken drainage plane across the parapet, so water is shed outward rather than trapped inside the wall. Terminating the membrane below the parapet cap and relying on the coping alone leaves a gap in the drainage plane at exactly the point most exposed to wind driven water. Installing the head flashing independently, with each trade sealing separately, creates a discontinuity where water can migrate between systems. Sealant alone at the coping joint is not a substitute for a continuous, lapped membrane connection and will eventually fail from movement and weathering.
  7. The correct answer is D (The lateral system already resists overturning elsewhere, so the beam only needs to transfer gravity shear at this joint.): because the building's lateral system already resists overturning at other locations, this connection is only responsible for transferring gravity shear, so a simple shear connection is the appropriate and more economical choice. A moment connection is not universally required everywhere a curtain wall anchor attaches to a structural member, since anchor movement is handled through slotted anchor detailing, not connection stiffness. Shear connections do not eliminate the curtain wall's need to accommodate structural deflection, that is addressed separately at the anchor. Fabrication cost alone is not the governing engineering reason for selecting a connection type in a properly designed lateral system.
  8. The correct answers are A, C, and D: feeder ampacity calculations must account for the continuous duty multiplier applied to loads running three hours or longer, ambient temperature derating when conductors are routed through a hot mechanical space, and the total connected load served by the feeder, since all three directly determine the required conductor size and overcurrent protection. Wall paint finish color has no electrical engineering relevance to conductor ampacity or derating. Manufacturer warranty period is a commercial term tied to the equipment itself and plays no role in sizing the feeder that serves it.
  9. The correct answer is D (The additional fixtures increase the total fixture unit demand on that branch, requiring a larger pipe to maintain adequate flow and pressure.): adding three lavatories raises the total fixture unit demand assigned to that branch, and a larger diameter pipe is needed to deliver adequate flow and maintain pressure to every fixture served. Branch pipe size is not governed by how many floors separate the restroom from the main riser. The added lavatories change hydraulic demand, not merely the visible finish material of the piping. Restroom branch piping is sized to the fixture unit load it actually serves, not simply matched to the diameter of the building's main water service.
  10. The correct answer is B (48 footcandles, calculated from 30,000 lumens times 0.6 times 0.8 divided by the 300 square foot room area.): multiplying 30,000 lumens by a coefficient of utilization of 0.6 and a light loss factor of 0.8 gives 14,400, and dividing by the 300 square foot room area (20 feet by 15 feet) yields 48 footcandles. The 24 footcandle result understates the calculation and does not follow from the given values. The 60 footcandle result overstates the illuminance the formula actually produces. The 36 footcandle result comes from misapplying the light loss factor to the area instead of the lumen output, which is not how the formula works.
  11. The correct answer is A (Continuous air barrier membrane at spandrel): the continuous membrane at the spandrel ties into the mullion air seal and maintains an unbroken air barrier plane across the transition. Interior gaskets seal individual joints but do not provide continuity across the full assembly. The rainscreen manages bulk water rather than air leakage, and the thermal break addresses conductive heat transfer, not infiltration. Detailers must trace the air barrier line through every transition to avoid a gap in the enclosure.
  12. The correct answer is B (Team resolves conflicts in coordination): once clash detection software flags conflicts, the design team meets to resolve them before the coordinated model can be finalized. The individual trade modeling and federation steps happen earlier, before any clashes can be detected. This sequence reflects the standard BIM based workflow used to reconcile MEP, structural, and architectural systems.
  13. The correct answer is B (Shear studs welded to top flange of beam): shear studs are welded to the beam's top flange and embedded in the concrete topping specifically to transfer horizontal shear between the two materials, allowing composite action. The metal deck forms the slab and spans between beams but does not itself create composite behavior. The concrete topping provides compressive strength, and fireproofing protects the steel, but neither creates the mechanical connection. Composite action increases the floor system's load capacity without increasing beam depth.
  14. The correct answer is C (Mullions erected and aligned plumb): the mullion frames must be erected and aligned plumb before glazing units can be set, since the glass panels rely on accurate mullion alignment to seat correctly and maintain uniform sightlines. Anchors are set into the slab edge earlier, before mullions are erected. Perimeter sealant is applied after glazing is installed, to complete the weathertight seal. Sequencing installation this way prevents misalignment that could compromise the structural connection and the water barrier.
  15. The correct answer is D (Flashing pan beneath rough opening): a flashing pan is sloped and detailed beneath the rough opening specifically to collect any water that penetrates the window assembly and direct it back out through the exterior wall surface. Weatherstripping reduces air infiltration at the operable sash, not bulk water at the rough opening. Locking hardware controls sash operation, and the glazing unit manages thermal and light performance. Without a properly detailed flashing pan, water can migrate into the framing and cause hidden moisture damage.
  16. The correct answer is A (Continuous insulation and air barrier added): once the structural framing and sheathing are established, the continuous insulation and air barrier layer is added next since it must be applied directly over the sheathing to maintain thermal and air control continuity. The cladding attachment system is detailed afterward, since it must penetrate or bypass the insulation layer in a controlled way. Finish cladding is specified last, once the supporting layers beneath it are resolved.
  17. The correct answer is C (Weep holes at base of cavity): weep holes are detailed at the base of the cavity specifically to let any water that collects behind the cladding drain out of the assembly. The air cavity itself allows drying through ventilation but does not by itself remove standing water. The water-resistive barrier sheds water toward the cavity, and attachment clips hold the panels in place, but neither provides a drainage path. Without weep holes properly located and kept clear, water can accumulate and deteriorate the substrate.
  18. The correct answer is B (Hoistway dimensions confirmed with vendor): the hoistway wall and pit dimensions must be confirmed with the elevator vendor before structural shaft framing is constructed, since the framing must be built to the exact clearances the equipment requires. Rail brackets and equipment are installed only after the shaft framing exists to receive them. Commissioning the cab, doors, and controls happens last, once all equipment is physically in place. Confirming vendor dimensions early prevents costly rework.
  19. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: a significant change in structural bay spacing, an abrupt change in building height or mass, wings meeting at an angle, and a long linear building exceeding a typical unbraced length are all recognized conditions that commonly require an expansion or seismic joint to accommodate differential movement between building sections. A change in interior paint color or restroom fixture brand between floors has no relationship to structural movement and does not indicate a need for a joint. These structural and geometric triggers are what the architect should specifically look for when reviewing a large or irregular floor plan. Identifying them early allows the joint to be integrated into the design rather than discovered as a problem later.
  20. The correct answer is C (Revise the detail to clearly show the end dam or upturned leg where the sill pan meets the jamb flashing): the detail must be revised to clearly show the end dam or upturned leg where the sill pan flashing meets the jamb flashing, since this is the specific condition that prevents water from migrating sideways into the wall cavity. Revising only the interior finish or the operable hardware addresses unrelated aspects of the window and does nothing to correct the water management gap at the jamb. Removing the sloped sill pan entirely would eliminate the feature managing incidental water at the sill rather than fix the actual missing detail at the jamb.
  21. The correct answer is A (Confirm the door and frame require a fire-protection rating appropriate to the wall assembly per code, and revise the schedule accordingly): a door located in a one-hour fire-rated corridor wall must have a door and frame assembly rated per the code's opening protection table, which for a 1-hour fire partition corridor wall requires a minimum 20-minute rated door and frame, not a rating that matches the full 1-hour wall, so the architect should confirm this requirement and revise the schedule to include the missing rating. Leaving the schedule unchanged assumes the wall rating alone is sufficient, when code requirements also separately govern the opening itself. Removing the door from the schedule, or adding a vague note leaving the rating determination to the contractor, both fail to establish the specific rated door and frame the opening actually requires.
  22. The correct answer is D (Coordinate with the mechanical engineer to confirm the required free area and revise the opening size accordingly): when the manufacturer's published free area is lower than what was originally assumed, the architect must coordinate with the mechanical engineer to confirm the actual required free area and revise the wall opening size to accommodate it. Proceeding with the original assumed size ignores documented catalog data that contradicts it. Changing only the specification without revising the drawn opening leaves the two documents describing different opening dimensions, and assuming the mechanical engineer will independently catch the discrepancy leaves a known conflict unaddressed.
  23. The correct answer is B (Revise the nosing projection dimension to comply with the code limit that always applies based on riser and tread proportions): the nosing projection must be revised to comply with the code limit that governs based on the stair's actual riser height and tread depth combination, since nosing dimensions are directly tied to those proportions in most model codes. Assuming the limit applies only to exterior stairs is incorrect, as interior stairs are subject to the same nosing projection requirements. Increasing the nosing further to create a visual illusion of depth moves the detail further from compliance, and removing the nosing ignores that it may also be required for tread visibility and slip resistance.
  24. The correct answer is C (The approach should be evaluated against whether a reasonably prudent architect would rely on catalog cuts for these conditions): whether relying on generic manufacturer catalog cuts is adequate should be evaluated against whether a reasonably prudent architect would consider that approach sufficient given the project's specific, non-standard conditions, the same professional standard of care applied throughout practice. Claiming catalog cuts are always adequate, or that project-specific drawings are always required for every detail regardless of complexity, both ignore that the appropriate level of detail depends on the actual conditions present. The architect's fee has no bearing on whether the documentation approach met the applicable standard of care.
  25. The correct answer is D (Yes, to always coordinate lighting, exposed ductwork, and conduit routing so no conflicts exist among these visible elements): even without a suspended ceiling, a reflected ceiling plan is necessary to coordinate lighting, exposed ductwork, and conduit routing so these visible elements do not conflict with each other, since an open ceiling design still exposes coordination problems if left undocumented. Assuming a reflected ceiling plan is not needed without a suspended system ignores that exposed conditions still require the same coordination a hidden ceiling would otherwise mask. Limiting the drawing to only a paint color note fails to address the actual coordination purpose the plan serves for an exposed ceiling design.
  26. The correct answers are A, B, C, and F: evaluating material substitutions against performance and code requirements, reviewing scope reduction or phasing with the owner, consistently revising specifications and drawings once a decision is finalized, and discussing cost-estimating method differences with the estimator are all appropriate ways to reconcile an estimate with the budget while keeping the documentation coordinated. Reducing structural member sizes below what the engineer's calculations require without informing the engineer of record, and deleting required life safety features without a code-compliant alternative, both compromise safety and code compliance rather than legitimately control cost. Legitimate value engineering targets cost drivers that can be adjusted without abandoning required performance or safety. Distinguishing these approaches from unsafe shortcuts is central to reconciling a budget responsibly.
  27. The correct answer is A (Revise every affected drawing and specification section together so the roof documentation reflects the new membrane system): substituting the roof membrane system affects drain heights, parapet flashing, and specification sections together, so every affected drawing and specification must be revised as a coordinated set to reflect the new system. Revising only the specification section ignores that drain heights and flashing profiles typically differ between built-up and single-ply systems. Leaving the roof plan or parapet detail unchanged, or leaving the original system's details for the contractor to adapt, both risk details that no longer match the actual membrane being installed.
  28. The correct answer is C (Confirm the scope reduction in writing with the owner, then revise all affected drawings and specifications to remove it): a late scope reduction should be confirmed in writing with the owner and then reflected by revising all affected drawings and specifications to remove the deleted item. Leaving the walkway shown unchanged because it was already detailed ignores the owner's actual direction to remove it from scope. Deleting it without written confirmation risks a dispute over whether the change was actually authorized, and leaving it partially shown on some sheets creates an inconsistent, confusing record of what is actually included.
  29. The correct answer is B (Confirm the requested hardware standard with the owner, then revise the hardware schedule and specification to match): the architect should confirm the requested hardware standard with the owner and then revise both the hardware schedule and specification consistently to reflect the agreed standard. Dismissing the comment as personal preference risks overlooking a legitimate campus standardization request the facilities manager is authorized to raise. Changing only the specification narrative while leaving the schedule unchanged, or recording the request only in meeting minutes without revising the documents, both fail to actually implement the confirmed change.
  30. The correct answer is A (Confirm the early packages are sufficiently complete and coordinated on their own, even though later packages remain in development): before issuing early foundation and structural steel packages on a fast-track project, the architect must confirm that those specific packages are sufficiently complete and internally coordinated on their own, since later packages will still be in development when they are released. Requiring early packages to match the completeness of a full design-bid-build set is not realistic for a fast-track sequence and misunderstands how phased issuance works. Assuming the construction manager has no role in reviewing early packages ignores the CM's active preconstruction involvement, and relying on verbal approval without any written record leaves no documented basis for having issued the packages.
  31. The correct answer is D (Yes, because a redesign requested for reasons outside the original scope after documents are substantially complete exceeds services): a lobby redesign requested for reasons unrelated to code, budget, or coordination, arriving after the documents are substantially complete, represents added scope beyond the original project as documented and typically justifies additional services. Assuming any request from an owner's staff member is automatically within scope ignores that the nature and timing of the request, not who makes it, determines whether it exceeds the original agreement. Conditioning the answer on whether the request happens to align with a code requirement misstates the actual basis for the determination, which is whether the work falls outside services already priced and performed.
  32. The correct answer is C (Document the curtain wall with design intent drawings and performance criteria appropriate to its complexity, coordinated with the specialty consultant): the complex unitized curtain wall warrants design intent drawings and performance criteria appropriate to its complexity, developed in coordination with the specialty consultant, while the simpler punched windows can be documented at a level appropriate to their lower complexity. Documenting both systems at an identical minimal level fails to address the curtain wall's actual complexity. Providing no design intent drawings for the curtain wall, or deferring both systems to subcontractor shop drawings with no architectural documentation, both abandon the architect's responsibility to establish design intent and performance requirements.
  33. The correct answer is B (To identify and resolve remaining conflicts between disciplines so the issued set presents one consistent, coordinated design): the primary purpose of a final coordination review before permit issuance is to identify and resolve remaining conflicts between disciplines so the issued set presents one consistent, coordinated design. Confirming sheet numbering conventions or specification font preferences addresses formatting rather than the substantive coordination the review is meant to catch. Reducing sheet count for its own sake, even when necessary information is lost, would work against the goal of a clear and complete document set rather than support it.
Case Study PDD_CD_C2

An architecture firm is completing the construction document (CD) phase for a six-story mixed-use building: ground-floor retail, four floors of residential units above, and one level of below-grade parking. The project is 85 percent through CD when the general contractor, brought on under a design-assist arrangement, proposes substituting the specified metal panel rainscreen cladding with a fiber cement panel system to reduce the construction cost by roughly six percent, citing current material lead times. Separately, the civil engineer issues a revised stormwater management plan that shifts an underground detention vault twelve feet north of its previous location, altering the routing of a site utility trench shown on the architect's civil-coordination sheet. Within the building itself, the architect is finalizing a wall section at the transition between two adjacent structural bays where the building's seismic joint occurs, and is also resolving how a fire-rated corridor partition should be depicted where it meets a storefront system at the retail level. The project uses an integrated BIM model shared with the structural and MEP consultants, and the owner has asked the architect to confirm, before documents are issued for permit, that recent scope changes are reflected consistently across the drawing set, the specifications, and any consultant sheets affected by the changes.

  1. The correct answer is B (Evaluate the proposed panel system against the original design intent, code requirements, and warranty performance before responding.): a proposed substitution during CD should first be evaluated against the performance, appearance, code, and warranty requirements the original system was specified to meet. Approving or rejecting the change without that review risks accepting a product that fails to match the design intent or misses a required rating. Once the evaluation is complete, the architect can advise the owner with a clear recommendation supported by the review findings. Issuing an addendum before the substitution has been vetted skips the design judgment the architect is responsible for.
  2. The correct answer is D (Update the coordination sheet to match the revised civil routing and confirm the change does not conflict with landscape or structural elements.): when a civil consultant revises underground utility routing, the architect must update the coordination sheet so the architectural drawings reflect the current condition and check the new routing against adjacent landscape and structural elements. Leaving outdated information on the architectural sheets creates a conflict between consultant drawings that can surface during construction. Removing utility references entirely would eliminate coordination the architect is responsible for maintaining. Waiting until after permit issuance delays the correction past the point where it can be resolved efficiently.
  3. The correct answer is A (The joint width, cover plate or seal condition, and how each wythe of the wall assembly is anchored independently across the joint.): a wall section at a seismic or expansion joint needs to show the joint width, the cover or seal condition that spans it, and confirm that each side of the wall assembly is anchored to its own structure so the assembly can move independently. Showing only the finish material omits the structural anchoring information a contractor needs to build the condition correctly. A generic catalog detail without project specific dimensions leaves the actual joint width and anchoring undefined. A plan reference alone cannot convey how the wall assembly itself responds to movement.
  4. The correct answer is C (Perform a coordinated QA review that cross checks the affected drawings, specification sections, and consultant sheets against the documented scope changes.): confirming that a scope change is reflected consistently requires a coordinated review that checks the specific drawings, specification sections, and consultant sheets the change actually touches, not a blanket assumption or disclaimer. Relying solely on each consultant's own review leaves the architect without direct knowledge that the change was captured correctly. Issuing the documents first and fixing conflicts through RFIs during construction shifts the cost and risk of the architect's own coordination failure onto the project. A general note about drawings superseding specifications does not resolve an actual conflict, it only assigns precedence after the fact.
  5. The correct answer is B (Bring the structural and envelope packages to a higher level of completeness earlier while interior packages are refined on a later schedule.): on a fast track project, the drawing packages tied to early trade releases, typically structural and envelope, need to reach a higher level of completeness sooner so fabrication can begin, while interior packages can be refined on a schedule that matches when that work is actually needed. Treating every package identically ignores the sequencing that fast tracking is meant to support. Waiting for full completion before releasing anything defeats the purpose of a fast track delivery method. Resolving finish decisions verbally during construction leaves no documented record of the design intent.
  6. The correct answer is A (Flag the conflict to both consultants so root zone requirements and the detention system's structural cover can be reconciled before documents are finalized.): the architect coordinates the overall site plan and is responsible for identifying where consultant drawings conflict, such as tree root zones overlapping a detention system's required structural cover, so the issue can be resolved through consultant coordination before the documents are finalized. Leaving the two consultants to resolve it independently risks a conflict going unnoticed since neither may be reviewing the other's drawing directly. Removing the trees without consulting the landscape architect discards design intent without exploring an alternative. Leaving the decision to the contractor during construction shifts a design coordination problem into the field.
  7. The correct answer is D (How the rated partition assembly terminates at the unrated storefront, including any required fire rated glazing, framing, or smoke seal to maintain the rating.): where a fire rated partition meets an unrated storefront system, the detail must show exactly how the rated assembly terminates and what fire rated glazing, framing, or smoke seal maintains the required rating across that transition. Assuming the storefront automatically meets the rating ignores that most storefront glazing and framing are not rated unless specifically detailed and specified as such. Showing only the finish material leaves the actual rated assembly condition undocumented. Leaving the transition to the contractor's discretion in the field creates a code compliance risk at a life safety element.
  8. The correct answer is C (Apply a consistent discipline based numbering convention, such as the Uniform Drawing System, across the architectural and consultant sheets.): applying a consistent discipline based numbering convention across the architectural and consultant sheets, following an industry standard such as the Uniform Drawing System, keeps the full set organized and lets drawings from different disciplines cross reference each other predictably. Allowing each consultant to use its own numbering system makes it harder to locate related information across disciplines. Numbering sequentially by completion date has nothing to do with discipline or content and would confuse anyone searching the set. Omitting sheet numbers entirely removes the primary way drawings are referenced from specifications and RFIs.
  9. The correct answers are A, C, and D: a significant scope change after permit issuance may require resubmission to the authority having jurisdiction, should be evaluated for whether it falls outside the architect's basic services under the owner agreement, and needs to be documented through a formal instrument with the affected drawings revised accordingly. Making the change directly in the field without updating the documents leaves no accurate record of what was actually built. Ignoring the request assumes permitted documents can never be revised, which is incorrect since permit amendments are routine. Directing the contractor to price the change before the scope is defined in the documents invites an inaccurate estimate.
  10. The correct answer is A (Revise the finish schedule, related details, and specification section to reflect the new material's actual profile and support requirements.): a material substitution that changes the edge profile and support requirements needs the finish schedule, the related detail drawings, and the specification section all updated so the documents reflect what will actually be built. Updating only the schedule name while leaving the details unchanged leaves the drawings describing a profile that no longer applies. A verbal note to the contractor creates no documented record and shifts a design decision to the fabricator. Waiting until substantial completion to reconcile the documents means the construction set never matched what was actually built.
  11. The correct answer is B (Whether the six percent slope exceeds the maximum running slope allowed for an accessible route and requires a ramp with landings instead.): a six percent running slope exceeds the maximum allowed for an accessible route, which is generally capped near five percent before the path is classified as a ramp requiring landings, handrails, and edge protection, so the architect must confirm this against the applicable accessibility standard and coordinate a revision with the civil engineer if needed. Assuming a connecting walkway is generally accessible ignores the actual slope requirement. Width alone does not determine whether a route is accessible when the running slope is out of compliance. A grading plan does not override accessibility requirements simply because it was issued first.
  12. The correct answer is D (Plan, section, and detail views dimensioning the nosing profile, tread and riser dimensions, and the required handrail extensions at the top and bottom landings.): a code compliant stair detail sheet needs plan, section, and detail views that dimension the nosing profile, tread and riser proportions, and the handrail extensions required at the top and bottom landings, since these are life safety dimensions that must be shown, not left to the specification or the fabricator alone. Referring only to the general code without dimensioning the actual condition leaves the contractor to interpret a requirement the architect is responsible for detailing. Leaving handrail resolution entirely to the fabricator removes architect oversight of a life safety element.
  13. The correct answer is A (Partition Type Detail): a partition type detail documents the full layered assembly of a specific wall condition, including stud type and spacing, gypsum board layers, insulation, and the fire and acoustic ratings that apply to that wall throughout the building. An expansion joint detail instead documents movement accommodation between materials, a stair section documents vertical circulation, and a louver head detail documents an opening condition rather than a wall assembly. Using the correct detail type lets the contractor build each unique wall condition exactly as rated. Mislabeling this content as a generic wall section risks omitting required fire and acoustic data.
  14. The correct answer is B (Owner Scope Addition): adding an entirely new wing expands the project beyond what was originally documented, which is a scope change initiated by the owner rather than a cost reduction, code requirement, or field discovery. A value engineering revision instead reduces cost without changing scope, a code compliance update responds to a regulatory requirement, and a field coordination change resolves a conflict discovered during construction. Categorizing the change correctly helps the architect determine whether additional services and fee adjustments are triggered. It also helps the team assess the schedule impact of restarting portions of the drawing set.
  15. The correct answer is D (Material Assembly Matrix): this tool is organized to cross reference every specified material against its fire rating and acoustic rating for each room type, giving the whole project team one consolidated reference. A finish schedule lists finishes by room without necessarily tabulating ratings, a door and hardware schedule addresses openings only, and a room finish legend is a symbol key rather than a rating summary. Consolidating this information in one matrix reduces the chance of an unrated assembly being installed in a location that requires a specific rating. It also speeds up code review since ratings are visible in a single location rather than scattered across sheets.
  16. The correct answer is C (CM at Risk, drawings developed with GC input): in this delivery method the general contractor is engaged early and reviews drawings for buildability and cost throughout development, rather than receiving a completed set as in design bid build. Design build instead releases drawings to the contractor in phases under a single combined contract, and IPD relies on a shared model rather than a traditional sequential drawing release. Selecting the correct documentation approach helps the architect plan when to solicit contractor feedback during drawing development. This early input can reduce costly redesign later in the project.
  17. The correct answer is C (Typical Detail, one and a half inch equals one foot): this is the largest scale in the table and provides enough resolution to clearly show stud spacing, individual gypsum board layers, and fastener locations, all of which are too small to read clearly at smaller scales. A building section at one eighth inch is meant to show overall vertical relationships, an enlarged plan at three eighths inch shows room layout, and a wall section at three quarter inch shows general assembly order without fine component detail. Choosing too small a scale for this content risks an unreadable or cluttered drawing. Matching scale to content is part of applying consistent documentation standards across the set.
  18. The correct answer is A (Coordinate Consultant Drawings): after floor plans, sections, and detail sheets are developed, the architectural drawings must be checked against structural, mechanical, electrical, and civil drawings to resolve conflicts before the set is considered final. Issuing the set before this coordination step risks releasing drawings with unresolved conflicts between systems. Establishing the sheet index happens earlier to organize the set, and completing plans, sections, and details happens before final consultant coordination can occur. This sequence reflects the standard of care expected when assembling a coordinated construction document set for permit and bid.
  19. The correct answer is C (Architect Issues CCD or ASI): once the review confirms the design intent impact, the architect issues the formal directive or clarification needed to authorize the change before the drawings themselves are revised. Revising drawings before issuing this instrument would leave the change undocumented and unauthorized in the official record. And the RFI submission has already occurred earlier in the sequence. Following this order keeps the documentation trail clear for cost, schedule, and design intent decisions made during construction.
  20. The correct answer is B (Confirm Grading and Utility Locations): after receiving the civil base plan, the architect must confirm grading and utility locations against the building design before architectural site features can be accurately overlaid. Overlaying architectural features before this confirmation risks placing entrances, ramps, or parking in conflict with unconfirmed grades or buried utilities. Issuing the coordinated site plan is the final step that follows both confirmation and overlay. This sequence supports accurate coordination between the architect and civil engineer on features that depend directly on grading and utility data.
  21. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: Part 1, General, of a specification section establishes administrative and procedural requirements that apply before products or execution are addressed, including related sections, submittal requirements, quality assurance provisions, and delivery, storage, and handling requirements. Manufacturer part numbers and specific material composition describe the actual product being furnished and belong in Part 2, Products. Field quality control procedures performed after installation describe work performed on site and belong in Part 3, Execution, not the general administrative content of Part 1.
  22. The correct answer is B (In the supplementary conditions or a Division 01 section addressing project-specific phasing requirements): project-specific requirements like maintaining an occupied wing during renovation, with defined noise and access restrictions, are documented in the supplementary conditions or a dedicated Division 01 phasing section so they become enforceable contract requirements. Noting the restriction only on a demolition sequence drawing does not establish it as a binding contractual obligation with defined hours and restrictions. An informal email sent after the contract is executed has no standing as part of the contract documents. Leaving the unmodified general conditions in place fails to add the project-specific phasing requirement the owner actually needs enforced.
  23. The correct answer is C (Division 09, Finishes, the division covering gypsum board assemblies and interior finish systems): MasterFormat places non-load-bearing metal stud framing and gypsum board partition assemblies within Division 09 because the section is organized around the finished interior wall system as a whole. Division 05 covers structural and miscellaneous metals fabrication, not interior non-load-bearing partition assemblies. Division 04 covers masonry unit construction, which is a different material and installation method than metal stud and gypsum board partitions. Division 06 covers wood, plastics, and composites, not gypsum board panel products.
  24. The correct answer is A (NFPA 285, the standard method of test for exterior wall assemblies containing combustible components): NFPA 285 evaluates the fire propagation characteristics of an entire multi-story exterior wall assembly containing combustible components like foam plastic insulation, which is required in addition to individual material or component ratings. ASTM E90 measures airborne sound transmission loss and has no bearing on fire propagation through an exterior wall. ASTM C1363 measures thermal performance of building assemblies, not fire spread behavior. ASTM E330 measures structural performance of windows and doors under wind load, not fire propagation through a combustible wall assembly.
  25. The correct answer is D (A bulletin or addendum updating the finish schedule and specification section, issued through proper channels): once specifications have already been issued for construction, final selections must be incorporated through a formal bulletin or addendum that updates both the drawings' finish schedule and the relevant specification section so the change becomes part of the contract documents. A verbal instruction during a site meeting has no documented standing and is easily disputed later. An informal punch list note near the end of construction is too late and not the proper mechanism for incorporating a design decision. Treating the change as an undocumented means and methods decision fails to formally record the owner's final selection.
  26. The correct answer is B (The general conditions of the contract for construction, which defines substantial completion): the general conditions define key contractual terms such as substantial completion, the point at which the owner may occupy or use the work for its intended purpose even though minor items remain incomplete. The technical specifications describe trade-specific product and installation requirements, not contractual milestone definitions. The invitation to bid is a procurement-phase document describing the anticipated schedule, not a defined contractual term. The supplementary conditions modify specific articles of the general conditions but do not themselves originate the substantial completion definition.
  27. The correct answer is C (The lack of competition may conflict with public bidding requirements for multiple qualified sources): naming a single manufacturer with no substitution language on a publicly bid project can conflict with public procurement rules that generally require competition among multiple qualified sources, exposing the specification to legal challenge. Bidders cannot automatically substitute a product without a defined procedure allowing them to do so. Naming one specific product and model number is the definition of a proprietary specification, not a performance specification, regardless of competition concerns. Limiting the specification to one manufacturer has no automatic effect on the contract sum and does not reduce project cost by itself.
  28. The correct answers are A, B, and D: material and product names, hardware set assignments for each opening, and fire-resistance ratings for the same assembly must all agree consistently across the drawings, specifications, and consultants' documents so the contractor prices and builds one coordinated scope of work. The architect's own professional liability insurance carrier and policy limits are a firm's internal business matter unrelated to construction document coordination. The contractor's internal daily labor scheduling and the owner's preferred meeting time are project administration and communication preferences, not technical content that must be coordinated across the drawings and specifications.
  29. The correct answer is D (A reference standard specification citing the applicable Architectural Woodwork Standards quality grade): citing a recognized industry reference standard, such as the Architectural Woodwork Standards quality grade, incorporates detailed tolerance and joinery requirements by reference without restating them, which is the most efficient specification approach for custom casework. A proprietary specification listing only approved fabricators establishes who can bid the work but does not by itself establish a quality grade. A descriptive specification restating every dimension and tolerance defeats the purpose of avoiding duplicated text. A performance specification requiring only owner satisfaction provides no measurable quality standard the fabricator can be held to.
  30. The correct answer is A (The instructions to bidders, within Division 00, Procurement Requirements): the instructions to bidders is the standard procurement document that establishes the exact bid submission date, time, location, and required bid security so every prospective bidder has consistent procedural information. The general conditions establish contractual terms for construction, such as contract time, not the bid submission procedure itself. Placing this information in a single trade's technical specification would limit its visibility to bidders reviewing only that section. The supplementary conditions address indemnification and insurance modifications, not bid submission logistics.
  31. The correct answer is C (The AISC Code of Standard Practice for Steel Buildings and Bridges): citing the AISC Code of Standard Practice incorporates recognized industry fabrication and erection tolerance practices for structural steel by reference, avoiding the need to restate an entire code within the specification. The International Building Code establishes minimum life-safety and structural requirements but is not the source for detailed steel fabrication tolerance practices. ASTM E119 is a fire-resistance test standard and has no relationship to steel fabrication tolerances. ACI 318 governs structural concrete design and construction, not structural steel fabrication practices.
  32. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: a complete bidding document package includes the Instructions to Bidders, the bid form the contractor submits, the drawings and specifications that define the work, and any addenda issued during bidding to clarify or modify those documents. A certificate of occupancy is issued by the building department after construction is complete and has no place in the bidding documents.
