As-built record drawings are produced by the contractor at the end of construction and are not part of the documents used to solicit bids.
  33. The correct answer is A (Issue a written addendum so every bidder receives the same clarification): any clarification or change to the bidding documents during bidding must be issued as a written addendum to all bidders, preserving a fair and level playing field. Responding privately by email to only one bidder gives that bidder information the others do not have, which is improper during bidding. Waiting until after award to resolve a known discrepancy through a change order needlessly increases cost and risk that could have been avoided before bids were submitted. Leaving the decision to a bidder's judgment invites inconsistent pricing among bidders and does not resolve the underlying conflict in the documents.
  34. The correct answer is D (Issue the addendum promptly and extend the bid date for bidders): when a significant addendum is issued close to the bid date, the architect should recommend extending the bid date so bidders have a fair opportunity to evaluate the change and adjust their pricing. Keeping the original date and expecting bidders to absorb a major last-minute change risks inaccurate or padded bids and potential claims later. Restarting the entire procurement is an excessive response when a straightforward date extension can resolve the issue. Allowing questions only after bids are submitted defeats the purpose of the addendum process, since bidders need the information before pricing their work.
  35. The correct answer is B (To identify contractors with the experience and capacity to bid): prequalification screens potential bidders on criteria such as relevant experience, bonding capacity, and financial stability so that only qualified firms are invited to submit bids, which is especially valuable on complex projects. Prequalification does not eliminate bonding requirements, which remain part of the bidding and contract requirements regardless. It does not set the contract price, since pricing is still determined through the competitive bid submissions. The owner still makes the final contract award decision, and prequalification only narrows the pool of eligible bidders rather than replacing owner approval.
  36. The correct answer is C (AIA Document A305, the Contractor's Qualification Statement form): A305 is the standardized form contractors complete to provide information about their experience, organization, personnel, equipment, and financial condition, which owners use to prequalify bidders. A701, Instructions to Bidders, governs how bids are submitted rather than collecting qualification data. A310, the Bid Bond, secures the bidder's commitment to enter the contract if awarded and has nothing to do with prequalification. A101 is the owner-contractor agreement used after a contractor has already been selected, not a tool for evaluating prospective bidders.
  37. The correct answer is A (The contractor may lack sufficient bonding capacity for a project this size): if a surety will not bond a contractor for the full value of the work, that signals the contractor may not have adequate financial capacity or bonding limits for a project of this scale, which is a legitimate basis for the owner to question the award. This limitation does not itself make the bid mathematically inconsistent, since bonding capacity and bid arithmetic are separate issues. Owners are not obligated to jump straight to the second lowest bidder without first investigating or allowing the contractor to resolve the bonding issue. Reducing project scope is not a required or typical response to a bonding capacity concern.
  38. The correct answer is D (Check the bidder's references and experience before making an award decision): being the low bidder does not by itself establish that a contractor is responsible, so the architect should recommend the owner verify references and relevant experience before award to confirm the contractor can perform work of this size and complexity. Awarding automatically ignores a legitimate concern about the contractor's capability. Disqualifying the bid without any investigation skips the due diligence step that could confirm or resolve the concern fairly. Asking for a further price reduction does not address the underlying question of whether the contractor can actually perform the work.
  39. The correct answer is C (The bidder may face cash flow problems that delay subcontractor payments): declining revenue combined with supplier liens on other projects is a warning sign of financial strain that could translate into payment delays and disruption on this project, which is a legitimate factor in evaluating a bidder's responsibility. Declining revenue is a warning sign of financial strain rather than something unlikely to affect the contractor's performance on this project. Liens filed by suppliers on other work are directly relevant to assessing financial stability, not irrelevant to it. Owners on private work generally retain discretion to consider financial history as part of determining whether a bidder is responsible.
  40. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: responsibility evaluations properly focus on a bidder's financial stability and bonding capacity, relevant experience, past performance and references, and current capacity to staff and complete the work. Considering a bidder's race, national origin, or ownership demographics as a basis for disqualification is unlawful discrimination and has no place in a legitimate responsibility determination. The appearance or color scheme of the bid submittal cover page has no bearing on whether the bidder can actually perform the contracted work.
  41. The correct answer is B (Treat the bid as non-responsive for failing a stated requirement): a bid that omits a required bid bond fails to comply with the mandatory requirements stated in the Instructions to Bidders and should be treated as non-responsive, regardless of price. Price alone does not determine acceptance, since the bid must first meet the stated procedural requirements to be considered. Allowing the bond to be submitted after award defeats the purpose of requiring it at bid time and undermines fairness to compliant bidders. Adjusting the bid amount to account for the missing bond is not a proper way to cure a non-responsive submission.
  42. The correct answer is A (The bid may be non-responsive for failing to price required alternates): when the bid form requires pricing for specific alternates and a bidder leaves them blank, the bid is incomplete and can be treated as non-responsive since the owner cannot rely on the missing information to make an informed award decision. Assuming the blank alternates carry no cost is speculative and unsafe for the owner to rely on. The architect has no authority to fill in pricing on a bidder's behalf, since that would misrepresent the bidder's actual offer. Ignoring the alternates undermines the purpose of requiring them, especially when the owner is depending on those prices to manage the budget.
  43. The correct answer is D (Reject the bid as non-responsive for arriving after the stated deadline): bids received after the deadline stated in the Instructions to Bidders are non-responsive and must be rejected to preserve fairness and integrity in the bidding process, regardless of the reason for the delay. Accepting a late bid alongside timely bids would be unfair to bidders who met the deadline. Deciding acceptance by a vote of other bidders is not a proper or authorized bid procedure. Opening the bid privately to assess competitiveness before deciding whether to accept it also compromises the fairness the deadline is meant to protect.
  44. The correct answer is A (Conduct a value engineering review that preserves the original design intent.): value engineering is a structured review of the design and specifications to find cost savings, such as alternate materials or systems, while preserving the project's essential function and design intent. Reducing structural loads below code minimums compromises safety and is not a legitimate cost-saving strategy. Informal subcontractor negotiation by the low bidder, often called bid shopping, is generally discouraged and does not involve the architect's design review. Allowing substitutions without architect or owner review risks changes that undermine design intent or code compliance without proper oversight.
  45. The correct answer is B (Rebid the project, possibly revising the schedule for better pricing): when high pricing reflects market conditions rather than design issues, rebidding, potentially with an adjusted schedule or expanded bidder list, gives the owner a chance to receive more competitive pricing without altering the design. Simply awarding to the lowest bidder still leaves the owner over budget without addressing the underlying issue. Canceling the project permanently is an extreme response when a targeted rebid may resolve the pricing problem. The architect has no obligation or basis to personally cover the funding gap between budget and bid price.
  46. The correct answer is A (They reduce the contract price if the owner removes that scope): a deductive alternate represents scope already included in the base bid that, if the owner accepts the alternate, is removed from the contract for a stated price reduction. Deductive alternates reduce cost when accepted, unlike an additive alternate, which increases the base bid price when selected instead. The base bid remains the foundation of the contract price, and alternates only adjust it rather than replacing it. Alternates are meant to be evaluated and decided at the time of bid award, not after the contract has already been executed.
  47. The correct answer is D (Written execution of the Change Order by the owner, contractor, and architect): A Change Order under A201 is a written instrument signed by the owner, contractor, and architect, and it modifies the contract sum, contract time, or both once fully executed. A single party's signature is not sufficient to bind all parties to the change, so A and B are both wrong. A verbal confirmation does not satisfy the requirement for a written, signed instrument, so C is wrong.
  48. The correct answer is C (Total float available before the activity affects the completion date): Total float is the amount of time an activity can be delayed without delaying the overall project completion date. Lag describes a required gap between dependent activities, not a delay allowance, so A is wrong. Duration variance measures a change in an activity's estimated length, not schedule flexibility, so B is wrong. Float is a scheduling characteristic of the activity itself, not a reserve controlled solely by the owner, so D is wrong.
  49. The correct answer is A (Reject the observed work as not conforming to the contract documents): A201 gives the architect authority to reject work that does not conform to the contract documents, which is a design-conformance determination rather than a means-and-methods instruction. Directing the specific technique would improperly dictate the contractor's means and methods, so B is wrong. Stopping the entire project is a remedy reserved for the owner under different contract provisions, not a routine architect action, so C is wrong. Approving payment for known nonconforming work would misrepresent the certification, so D is wrong.
  50. The correct answer is B (The completion date is unaffected since the delay is within available float): Because the activity has ten days of float and the delay only consumes four of those days, the activity remains non-critical and the project completion date does not shift. A delay only pushes the completion date when it exceeds the available float on that path, so A is wrong. Only the float actually consumed matters, not the entire float reserve, so C is wrong. Schedule impact analysis does not require renegotiating the contract sum, so D is wrong.
  51. The correct answer is C (A request for information seeking clarification of the contract documents): An RFI is the standard mechanism a contractor uses to ask the architect for clarification or interpretation when a field condition conflicts with or is unclear in the contract documents. A Construction Change Directive is issued by the owner and architect to direct changed work, not submitted by the contractor to ask a question, so A is wrong. A certificate of substantial completion addresses project completion status, not a field clarification, so B is wrong. A notice of claim formally asserts entitlement to time or cost, which is a separate and later step than simply requesting clarification, so D is wrong.
  52. The correct answer is D (That the work has progressed to the point indicated with acceptable quality): Under A201, the architect's certification represents a professional judgment, based on observations and the data in the application, that the work has progressed to the indicated point and the quality generally conforms to the contract documents. The architect's certification does not guarantee that subcontractors have actually been paid, so A is wrong. The architect cannot certify conditions concealed from view without exhaustive or continuous inspection, so B is wrong. Certification never constitutes approval of the contractor's means and methods, so C is wrong.
  53. The correct answer is B (Front-load early payments by shifting value into activities completed first): Inflating early line items relative to their real cost, while underpricing later ones, is a classic front-loading tactic that accelerates the contractor's cash flow ahead of actual progress. It does not simplify review, and in fact an architect should scrutinize an unbalanced schedule of values more closely, so A is wrong. Owners do not request imbalanced pricing to simplify review, so C is wrong. Front-loading does not by itself reduce the retainage percentage applied to the contract, so D is wrong.
  54. The correct answer is A (A punch list of items to be completed or corrected before final payment): The punch list documents remaining incomplete or defective work identified near substantial or final completion so the contractor can address it before final payment is released. A schedule of values is prepared earlier in the project to allocate cost to work items, not to track closeout deficiencies, so B is wrong. A construction change directive addresses changed scope and pricing, not a closeout deficiency list, so C is wrong. A certificate of insurance documents coverage, unrelated to physical completion status, so D is wrong.
  55. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: Identifying the critical path requires accurate activity durations, the logical sequencing relationships between activities, any required lag or lead time, and a defined start date from which the network is calculated. Formatting choices like a color scheme have no bearing on the network calculation, so C is wrong. A font choice similarly has no effect on identifying the critical path, so E is wrong.
  56. The correct answer is C (No, because the contractor is solely responsible for safety precautions and programs): A201 makes the contractor solely responsible for initiating, maintaining, and supervising safety precautions and programs in connection with the work, and the architect has no duty to inspect or enforce them during site visits. A general site visit does not create an inspection obligation for safety-specific conditions, so A is wrong. The architect never holds broad responsibility for the contractor's safety program under A201, so B is wrong. The outcome is the same as it would be even if the architect had visited that day, so D is wrong.
  57. The correct answer is B (The contractor, because submittal review does not relieve it of responsibility for errors): A201 is explicit that the architect's review of submittals does not relieve the contractor of responsibility for deviations or errors and omissions in the submittal, even if the architect did not catch them. The architect's review is for the limited purpose of design conformance, not a comprehensive check that shifts liability, so A is wrong. The owner's selection of a system does not transfer responsibility for fabrication accuracy, so C is wrong. Responsibility is not automatically split evenly under the contract's submittal provisions, so D is wrong.
  58. The correct answer is A (Float belongs to the project as a whole rather than to either single party): The prevailing industry and scheduling-specification position treats float as a shared project resource available on a first-used basis, not the property of either the owner or the contractor alone. Treating float as exclusively the contractor's would let the contractor consume it and then claim delay damages for owner-caused changes that should have absorbed available float, so B is wrong. Treating float as exclusively the owner's similarly misstates standard practice, so C is wrong. Ownership of float does not depend on network sequence position, so D is wrong.
  59. The correct answer is D (A Construction Change Directive ordering the change pending later price agreement): A Construction Change Directive is a written order signed by the owner and architect directing a change in the work when the parties have not yet agreed on an adjustment to the contract sum or time. A Certificate for Payment addresses periodic payment amounts, not authorization for changed scope, so A is wrong. A punch list tracks closeout deficiencies rather than directing new work, so B is wrong. A schedule of values revision reallocates existing pricing and does not itself authorize a change in scope, so C is wrong.
  60. The correct answer is B (Concurrent delay, since two independent causes affect the critical path simultaneously): Concurrent delay occurs when two or more independent causes, at least one attributable to each party, delay the critical path during the same period, which typically complicates entitlement to time extensions and damages. Float erosion describes float being consumed over time, not the simultaneous overlap of two delay causes, so A is wrong. Constructive acceleration refers to a contractor being compelled to speed up work without a formal change order, which is not what is described here, so C is wrong. Pacing delay refers to a contractor deliberately slowing non-critical work to match a critical delay, not this scenario, so D is wrong.
  61. The correct answer is C (To the owner and contractor, and retained in the architect's project record): Site observation reports are typically distributed to both the owner and contractor and kept in the architect's project file, so all parties are informed of observed conditions and any noted deficiencies. Keeping the report solely internal defeats its purpose of keeping the owner informed, so A is wrong. Sending it only to the contractor withholds important information the owner is entitled to receive, so B is wrong. Field reports are project records, not building department submittals, unless a specific code issue independently requires notification, so D is wrong.
  62. The correct answer is A (The architect generally has no independent contractual authority to stop the work): Under A201, the authority to order a stoppage of work generally rests with the owner in specific enumerated circumstances, not with the architect acting on its own initiative for a safety condition. The architect does not hold unilateral stop-work authority over general conditions observed on site, so B is wrong. The architect is not contractually obligated to halt the project for every observed issue, since the contractor bears safety responsibility, so C is wrong. Stopping work is never conditioned on the contractor's consent, since that would be an odd allocation of authority, so D is wrong.
  63. The correct answers are A, B, C, and E: A meaningful schedule update comparison examines actual dates achieved, reported percent complete, any shift in the critical path, and the remaining duration and logic for unfinished work. The architect's own insurance premium has no relationship to construction progress monitoring, so D is wrong. Individual payroll records are confidential contractor business information unrelated to schedule performance, so F is wrong.
  64. The correct answer is D (Check general conformance of the proposed work with the design concept): Shop drawing review under A201 is expressly limited to checking for conformance with the design concept and the information given in the contract documents, not a comprehensive technical or code compliance review. Bonding capacity is unrelated to a design-conformance review of a submittal, so A is wrong. Labor rate comparisons fall outside the architect's submittal review scope entirely, so B is wrong. The architect's review does not constitute a code compliance certification, so C is wrong.
  65. The correct answer is B (Excusable delay, since it results from conditions beyond either party's control): Delays caused by events like severe weather beyond either party's control are typically classified as excusable, generally entitling the contractor to a time extension. Calling it inexcusable would be incorrect, since excusable delays are recognized precisely for events like this, so A is wrong. Excusable does not automatically mean compensable, since many weather-related excusable delays extend time without additional payment, so C is wrong. Nothing in the scenario indicates an overlapping contractor-caused delay, so D is wrong.
  66. The correct answer is A (Record drawings, maintained by the contractor and updated throughout construction): Contractors are typically required to maintain an updated set of record, or as-built, drawings reflecting actual field conditions and changes as the project proceeds, which are later transmitted to the owner. Shop drawings are prepared and submitted for review before fabrication, not marked up progressively to reflect completed field changes, so B is wrong. A schedule of values allocates cost to line items and has nothing to do with as-built conditions, so C is wrong. Certificates for payment document payment amounts rather than field conditions, so D is wrong.
  67. The correct answer is C (The architect, who renders interpretations of the contract documents' requirements): A201 assigns the architect responsibility for interpreting and deciding matters concerning performance under and the requirements of the contract documents. Legal counsel is not the first line of interpretation for a routine drawing ambiguity encountered in the field, so A is wrong. The contractor cannot unilaterally interpret its own ambiguity to its advantage, since that role belongs to the architect, so B is wrong. Building officials enforce code compliance, not the intended meaning of the design team's documents, so D is wrong.
  68. The correct answer is B (The completion date is pushed back by approximately two days): Because the activity is on the critical path with zero float, any delay to it flows directly through to the project completion date, so a two-day delay generally pushes completion back roughly two days absent mitigation. A delay to a zero-float critical activity always affects completion regardless of its apparent size relative to the whole schedule, so A is wrong. There is no float on this activity to reference, since it has already been stated as zero, so C is wrong. The completion date impact is realized through the schedule logic, not delayed until package completion, so D is wrong.
  69. The correct answer is D (It helps prevent front-loaded billing that overpays the contractor relative to progress): Reviewing the schedule of values for reasonable line-item pricing helps the architect catch front-loading, which would otherwise let the contractor be paid disproportionately ahead of actual completed work. The architect has no authority to renegotiate the contractor's bid price through this review, so A is wrong. There is no requirement tying contractor profit margin to the architect's design fee, so B is wrong. Payment bond qualification is determined by the surety, not by the architect's schedule of values review, so C is wrong.
  70. The correct answer is C (Lag time, a required delay between the finish of one activity and the start of its successor): Lag is a deliberate, required waiting period built into the logical relationship between two dependent activities, distinct from float, which represents schedule flexibility. Float describes how much an activity can slip without affecting downstream work or completion, not a mandatory waiting period, so A and B are both wrong. Crashing refers to adding resources to shorten a schedule, which is unrelated to a required waiting period, so D is wrong.
  71. The correct answer is A (The contractor, who coordinates all portions of the work under the contract): A201 assigns the contractor responsibility for coordinating all portions of the work, including the sequencing and coordination of its subcontractors, unless the contract documents provide otherwise. The architect's site visits are for general familiarity with progress, not for coordinating trade sequencing, so B is wrong. The owner does not typically manage subcontractor coordination directly under the standard contract structure, so C is wrong. The structural engineer's role is design-related and does not extend to resolving trade coordination conflicts, so D is wrong.
  72. The correct answers are A, B, and C: the contractor routinely transmits shop drawings, product data, and samples to the architect for review under Article 3.12 of AIA A201; applications for payment are a separate process governed by Article 9 and are not classified as a Submittal. Internal profit and overhead calculations are confidential business information the contractor is not required to share with the architect, so D is wrong. Certified payroll reports document wage compliance, not design conformance, and are not a project submittal reviewed by the architect, so E is wrong. Private correspondence between the contractor and its subcontractors is likewise not a required project submittal, so F is wrong.
  73. The correct answers are A, B, C, and F: shop drawings, product data, samples, and independent test reports are all standard submittal categories reviewed by the architect to confirm general conformance with the design intent. Certificates of insurance are typically reviewed administratively by the owner or its representative rather than as a design submittal. An as-built survey of adjacent properties is not a design conformance submittal and falls outside the typical submittal review process.
  74. The correct answer is B (Approved as noted, subject to the finish sample confirmation): this stamp is used when the submittal is generally acceptable but carries minor notations or conditions, such as requiring a follow-up sample, that the contractor can incorporate without resubmitting the entire item. Approved would be used only if no conditions or corrections were needed. Revise and resubmit is reserved for submittals with more significant deficiencies requiring a full resubmission before proceeding, and rejected indicates the submittal does not conform and cannot be used at all.
  75. The correct answer is C (Revise and resubmit the drawings to correct the identified deficiencies): significant deficiencies, such as missing weld symbols and connections that conflict with the contract documents, require the contractor to correct and resubmit the entire submittal rather than proceed with minor notations. Approved as noted is reserved for minor issues that do not require full resubmission, and approved would be inappropriate given the substantive nonconformance identified. No exceptions taken is not a standard AIA review stamp and is typically used by other parties under different documentation systems.
  76. The correct answer is D (The contractor, who must furnish sufficient data supporting the claimed equivalence): the contractor requesting a substitution bears the burden of demonstrating, with sufficient supporting data, that the proposed product is equal in quality, function, and performance to the specified item. The architect is not obligated to independently test the substitute but reviews the data the contractor provides, and the owner has no burden to disprove the substitution since the request originates with the contractor. Manufacturer certification alone, without contractor-submitted comparative data, is not sufficient to satisfy this burden.
  77. The correct answer is C (To confirm the visual appearance and workmanship standard before full application): a mock-up or field sample establishes an agreed-upon benchmark for appearance, texture, and workmanship that subsequent installed work must match. It has no direct role in establishing invoice amounts, which are governed by the schedule of values and pay applications, and structural load testing is unrelated to a finish mock-up's purpose. Warranty documentation is a separate closeout submittal, not the function of a mock-up.
  78. The correct answer is A (The architect may simply return the submittal to the contractor without action): the contractor is contractually required to review and approve submittals before forwarding them to the architect, and the architect may return unreviewed submittals without taking action on their content. The architect's review obligation is conditioned on prior contractor review, so it is not an absolute requirement regardless of contractor action. A skipped review step does not create an automatic approval, and submittal review remains the architect's responsibility, not the owner's.
  79. The correct answer is B (A Request for Information submitted to the architect asking for clarification of an ambiguity): an RFI is the proper vehicle for the contractor to formally ask the architect to resolve an apparent conflict or ambiguity between the specifications and drawings before continuing work. A Change Order presumes a scope change has already been agreed upon, which is premature here. A Construction Change Directive is used to direct a change without agreement on cost or time, not to answer a document conflict question, and a Certificate of Substantial Completion applies at project completion and has no bearing on resolving a document conflict.
  80. The correct answer is D (A Construction Change Directive issued by the owner and architect before cost or time is agreed): a CCD is specifically designed to authorize the contractor to proceed with directed work when the owner and contractor have not yet agreed on the cost or time adjustment, preserving project schedule while the impact is resolved. A Request for Information cannot direct a change in scope of work, and an Architect's Supplemental Instruction is limited to changes with no cost or time impact, which does not fit an unforeseen condition requiring demolition. A submittal transmittal is an administrative cover document with no authority to direct changed work.
  81. The correct answers are A, B, D, and F: a Construction Change Directive can be issued and signed by the owner and architect without the contractor's agreement on cost or time, may direct changes affecting the contract sum or time, and once cost is later agreed upon it typically converts into a Change Order. The contractor's signature is not required for the CCD to be valid and effective, and CCDs are used during active construction to address unresolved changes, not after substantial completion.
  82. The correct answer is B (A minor change in the work ordered directly by the architect): the architect has authority under A201 to order minor changes in the work that do not involve an adjustment to the contract sum or contract time, without owner or contractor signature. A Change Order is unnecessary and inappropriate because no cost or time impact exists, and a Construction Change Directive is reserved for changes where cost or time impact is anticipated but unresolved. Simply responding to an RFI would clarify intent but is not the formal instrument used to direct the physical change.
  83. The correct answer is C (To verify the placed concrete actually meets the specified strength before proceeding): test reports confirm that installed materials actually achieve the performance criteria specified in the contract documents before subsequent work depends on that performance. Delivery scheduling is a logistics matter unrelated to the purpose of a strength test submittal, and payment application amounts are established through the schedule of values, not test reports. While the contractor may also use test data internally, the contractual purpose is to document conformance to the owner and architect.
  84. The correct answer is A (Specifically and clearly inform the architect in writing of the deviation): the contractor is required to notify the architect in writing of any deviations from the contract documents at the time of submittal so the architect can evaluate and act on the deviation. Submitting without comment risks the deviation being overlooked, which the contract explicitly does not permit, and the contractor has no authority to unilaterally revise the contract documents. Owner involvement is not required for routine submittal deviations, which are handled through the architect's review process.
  85. The correct answer is D (Tracking the item on a submittal schedule with target review and delivery dates): a submittal schedule identifies critical or long lead items early and establishes target dates for submission, review, and delivery so the design and construction team can proactively manage the sequence. Waiting until the shaft is complete would compress the schedule further and increase delay risk, and verbal assurances provide no documented tracking and are unreliable for schedule coordination. Excluding long lead items from the log would remove visibility into exactly the items most likely to cause delay.
  86. The correct answer is A (Reject the request as incomplete and require supporting comparative performance data): a substitution request must include sufficient data demonstrating that the proposed product is equivalent in performance, quality, and function, and a brochure alone does not satisfy that burden. Approving without adequate data would improperly shift acceptance risk without justification, and increasing the contract sum automatically has no basis without a properly substantiated and negotiated request. While the owner may ultimately be consulted, the architect first evaluates the technical adequacy of the submitted request.
  87. The correct answer is C (Potential schedule delay along with possible contractor claims for added cost): unanswered RFIs can hold up work that depends on the requested clarification, creating schedule impacts and potential contractor claims for the resulting delay costs. Delayed responses do not inherently improve trade coordination and more often disrupt it, and RFI delays have no bearing on automatically approving unrelated substitution requests. Submittal review remains a separate, ongoing contractual obligation regardless of RFI response timing.
  88. The correct answer is B (Note the conflict and require resolution consistent with the specifications): the architect should flag the discrepancy and direct the contractor to resolve the submittal so it conforms to the governing project specifications, since standard manufacturer data does not override project-specific requirements. Approving the submittal as-is would allow a nonconforming product to proceed, and a stop work order is a disproportionate response to a submittal discrepancy that can be resolved through the review process. Specifications are binding contract documents, not advisory suggestions, so ignoring the conflict is not appropriate.
  89. The correct answers are A, B, D, and E: a well-supported substitution request includes comparative performance data, the cost and schedule impact, samples where relevant, and confirmation that the substitute meets applicable code and warranty requirements. A supervisor's personal recommendation letter is not objective technical or contractual evidence of equivalence, and internal payroll records have no bearing on evaluating whether a substitute product is functionally and qualitatively equal to the specified item.
  90. The correct answer is D (To allow accurate evaluation of the color and finish under real conditions): physical samples on actual substrate more accurately represent how a finish will appear in real lighting and material conditions than a printed chip, reducing the risk of an unexpected result once fully applied. Requiring samples does not reduce overall submittal tracking, since it is itself a tracked submittal, and a finish sample does not eliminate the potential need for a larger field mock-up for texture or application technique. Pricing is addressed through the schedule of values, not a finish sample.
  91. The correct answer is B (A Change Order is executed reflecting the newly agreed cost and time): once the owner and contractor reach agreement on the cost and time impact of a directed change, a Change Order is typically executed to formally memorialize and close out the terms, converting the CCD's interim authorization into a fully agreed contract modification. Leaving the CCD as the sole governing document would not capture the final agreed terms, and an RFI is a clarification tool with no role in closing out a cost negotiation. The architect does not unilaterally set final pricing, since it must be agreed upon by owner and contractor.
